15th Sunday in Ordinary Time A Pilgrim's Journey: The Imperatives for the Future (Joseph Lobo Audio Lectures) From Ashes to Alleluia (Lenten Talk) Hubert Dreyfus Heidegger Being and Time lecture Orientation final draft Apdex The Parable of the Quiet Hare Talk 1 and 2 MSC The Priest's Paschal Journey with Christ The Spirit of Discernment: Walking Together in Divine Wisdom This Is My Beloved Son

15th Sunday in Ordinary Time

Overview

Drawing on the readings for the 15th Sunday in Ordinary Time the central theme is clear: God’s Word is powerful, but the fruit depends on the condition of our hearts. Isaiah assures us that God’s Word never returns empty, Matthew explains why people respond differently, and Romans reminds us that all creation is waiting for the final harvest of God’s Kingdom.

Homily

15th Sunday in Ordinary Time (Year A)

Theme: “Wi-Fi, Farming, and the Heart”

Introduction

My dear brothers and sisters,

Have you ever noticed something strange?

If the Wi-Fi is slow, everyone becomes a scientist.

“Maybe it’s the router.”

“No, no... it must be the cable.”

“Switch it off... wait ten seconds... now switch it on.”

Even the grandmother, who has never used Google, suddenly becomes an IT expert!

But when the priest says, “How is your prayer life?”

Silence...

Nobody says, “Father, maybe my spiritual router needs restarting!”

We worry when the internet doesn’t connect.

But we rarely worry when our hearts don’t connect with God.

Today’s Gospel asks only one question:

How is the signal in your heart?

A Story

Gaur Gopal Das often tells simple stories with profound truths. Let me share one in that spirit.

A grandfather had two grandsons.

Every morning he would give each one a handful of seeds.

The first grandson carefully prepared the soil. He removed stones, pulled out weeds, watered the ground and patiently waited.

The second grandson simply threw the seeds wherever he liked.

Some landed on the road.

Some on rocks.

Some among thorny bushes.

After two months, the grandfather came back.

The first boy had a beautiful garden.

The second had an empty basket.

The younger grandson complained,

“Grandpa, these seeds are useless!”

The grandfather smiled and said,

“My child, don’t blame the seed. Look at where you planted it.”

Isn’t that exactly what Jesus says today?

The problem is never the seed.

The problem is the soil.

The Four Hearts

Jesus does not speak about four kinds of seeds.

He speaks about four kinds of hearts.

1. The Road

Some hearts are like highways.

The Word of God lands...

...and immediately the traffic of life runs over it.

Mass finishes.

By the time we reach the church gate...

“What shall we eat?”

“Did India win?”

“Where are the car keys?”

The Gospel has already disappeared.

Jesus says the birds carry away the seed.

Today the birds have become mobile phones.

Sometimes the first thing we check after Communion is not our conscience...

...but our WhatsApp!

2. Rocky Ground

These are enthusiastic Christians.

They attend retreats.

They buy five Bibles.

They underline everything.

They post inspirational quotes.

Then one small problem comes.

One criticism.

One unanswered prayer.

One disappointment.

And suddenly...

“God doesn’t love me anymore.”

Faith without roots cannot survive the heat.

A tree does not become strong during the storm.

It becomes strong before the storm.

3. Thorny Ground

This is perhaps the biggest danger today.

Jesus names the thorns.

Not great sins.

Not terrible crimes.

Simply...

Worries.

Riches.

Distractions.

Notice something.

Thorns don’t kill the plant overnight.

They slowly squeeze the life out of it.

Nobody wakes up one morning and says,

“Today I will stop loving God.”

It happens little by little.

A little more money.

A little more status.

A little more entertainment.

Until there is no room left for God.

4. Good Soil

Good soil is not perfect soil.

It is prepared soil.

Farmers know something we city people forget.

The harvest is not produced on the day of sowing.

There are days of watering.

Days of waiting.

Days when nothing seems to happen.

Yet beneath the surface, life is growing.

The same happens with God’s Word.

Sometimes we think,

“I’ve been praying for years. Nothing is changing.”

God says,

“Keep watering.”

Isaiah reminds us that just as rain never falls without nourishing the earth, God’s Word never fails to accomplish His purpose.

St. Paul Gives Us Hope

Paul tells us that creation itself is groaning.

Even nature is waiting for redemption.

The world feels broken.

Families struggle.

Young people face anxiety.

Communities experience conflict.

Yet Paul says these sufferings are like labour pains.

A mother in labour cries.

But her cry is not the cry of death.

It is the cry before new life.

Christian hope is not pretending there is no pain.

Christian hope believes that God is already preparing the harvest.

A Little Humour

A priest once asked a little boy after Mass,

“What did you learn from today’s Gospel?”

The boy answered,

“Father, Jesus wants us to become dirt!”

The priest was shocked.

“No! No! Good soil!”

The boy replied,

“Exactly, Father. Good dirt!”

Sometimes children understand the Gospel better than theologians.

God is not asking us to become famous.

He is asking us to become fertile.

Practical Challenge

This week, don’t try to change your whole life.

Just do three small things.

Every morning, read one verse of the Gospel.

Every evening, spend five minutes in silence.

Every day, remove one thorn.

Perhaps resentment.

Perhaps endless scrolling.

Perhaps unnecessary gossip.

Prepare the soil.

God will take care of the harvest.

Conclusion

My dear brothers and sisters,

God is still sowing.

He has not stopped speaking.

The real question is not,

“Is God speaking?”

The question is,

“What kind of soil am I today?”

If our hearts become good soil,

God promises something extraordinary.

Not just thirty-fold.

Not just sixty-fold.

But a hundred-fold.

May this Eucharist soften every hardened heart, deepen every shallow heart, clear every thorn-filled heart, and make each one of us fertile soil where the Word of God bears abundant fruit for the life of the world.

Amen.

Drawing on the readings for the 15th Sunday in Ordinary Time the central theme is clear: God’s Word is powerful, but the fruit depends on the condition of our hearts. Isaiah assures us that God’s Word never returns empty, Matthew explains why people respond differently, and Romans reminds us that all creation is waiting for the final harvest of God’s Kingdom.

A Pilgrim's Journey: The Imperatives for the Future (Joseph Lobo Audio Lectures)

Overview

Course Topic: Synodality: A Pilgrim’s Journey & The Imperatives for the Future

Instructor’s Note: This material covers the theological, social, and political dimensions of "Synodality." It moves beyond the definition of Synodality within the Catholic Church to explore its application as a necessary "pervasive ethos" for the wider world.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Module 1: Status and Scope of the Synodal Journey

1.1 Current Status

• The Question: Are we at the "pinnacle" of the Synodal journey?

• The Reality: We have likely not even reached the beginning of the actual journey by any stretch of human imagination. While formal forums (like the October sessions) may end, the actual journey has barely taken a few initial steps.

• Global Variance: There is a lack of robustness in the journey globally; some parts of the world have yet to "warm up" to the idea.

1.2 The Scope

• Beyond the Church: Synodality is not merely a "pious principle" for Catholics. It is a vital necessity for the wider human society to ensure human dignity, justice, liberty, and fraternity.

• Secular Application: It serves as an antidote to modern societal ills such as fundamentalism, majoritarianism, and autocratic populism.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Module 2: The Eight Imperatives of Synodality

These are the eight specific contexts (imperatives) that make Synodality not an option, but a necessity today.

A. Social & Philosophical Imperatives

1. The Contextual Imperative (The Need to Listen)

• Specification: Listening is a social act essential for the existence of society, not just a personal habit.

• Key Metaphors:

◦ Tower of Babel: A symbol of the lack of mutual listening leading to confusion.

◦ Pentecost: A community of strangers united through mutual listening.

• Current Crisis: The world is turning a "deaf ear" to the cry of nature, the poor, and the indigenous, leading to carnage (e.g., Ukraine, Gaza).

2. The Post-Colonial/Post-Modern Imperative

• Concept: Post-modern consciousness rejects "Meta-narratives" (Grand Narratives).

• Specification: A meta-narrative claims one theory explains everything (e.g., Western doctrine, specific social theories). Synodality "debunks authoritarianism" because truth must be grasped dialogically, not imposed by a few.

• The Center vs. Periphery:

◦ Critique: The "periphery" (colonized nations) now critiques the "center" (dominant powers).

◦ The Paradox: The periphery often uses the tools of the center (language, technology, AI) to critique it, while the center devises new forms of control (algorithms, robotics). Synodality proposes a "mutually beneficial negotiation" rather than perpetual conflict.

3. The Imperative of Subaltern Consciousness

• Key Question: "Can the Subaltern Speak?" (Ref: Gayatri Spivak).

• Specification: "Subaltern" refers to the marginalized (e.g., unorganized labor, women, minorities). Spivak argues they cannot speak because no one listens.

• The Synodal Response: Violence (burning houses, lynching) occurs because dominant groups feel threatened by the rising voices of the subaltern. Synodality offers a constructive way to listen to these muted voices without bloodshed.

B. Theological & Missiological Imperatives

4. The Theological Imperative

• Shift in Theology: Theology itself is a product of a synodal process. An "outgoing Church" requires an "outgoing theology".

• Example: Theologians must dialogue with:

◦ People on the streets.

◦ Non-Christian religions.

◦ Domains of knowledge other than the Bible.

• Impact: This shatters the "false security" of theologians who believe they possess the absolute truth and simply need to "teach" others.

5. The Mission Practice Imperative

• Key Concept: The Holy Spirit is active in all cultures, not just the Church.

• Terminology Specification:

◦ Missio ad gentes: Mission to the nations (Traditional view).

◦ Missio cum gentibus: Mission with the nations (Collaborative).

◦ Missio in gentibus: Mission among the nations (Immanent presence).

• Requirement: We cannot ignore the wisdom systems of other nations; we must build theology with them.

C. Political & Structural Imperatives

6. The Religio-Political Imperative

• The Threat: "Hypocrisies" hiding behind the garb of democracy. Leaders demand absolute fidelity, often colored by religious nationalism.

• Synodal Challenge: Synodality challenges "blind loyalty" (leaders asking followers to die for them) by asking leaders to "walk alongside" the people instead.

7. The Democracy Imperative

• Critique of Majoritarianism: Democracy today is often reduced to "numbers" (51% ruling over 49%), which allows for the tyranny of the majority.

• The Synodal Difference: Synodality introduces Discernment. It is not just about counting votes but about equitable ownership and empathy.

• Key Insight: "The down-trodden are not a natural phenomenon but a social product." Society creates poverty; therefore, society must reverse it.

8. The Institutional Imperative

• The Goal: Renewal of institutions to prevent stagnation.

• The Dual Process (Hermeneutics):

1. Hermeneutics of Suspicion: Constantly asking, "Is the current way of doing things still relevant?" (Jesus saying, "You have heard it said... but I say to you").

2. Hermeneutics of Retrieval: Retrieving the original charism/spirit of the foundation in a new, relevant form.

• Accountability: Synodality reduces corruption by demanding accountability from power-holders.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Module 3: Obstacles and Resistance

3.1 Positive Signs

• There is a clear "demand for change," which is a positive indicator of awareness.

• Increased discourse, seminars, and participation (especially in Latin America).

3.2 Root Causes of Resistance

1. Clericalism: A deep-rooted fear that Synodality means a "loss of power" or control.

2. The "Three Roles" Confusion: In the Old Testament, the King (ruler), Prophet (critic), and Priest (ritualist) were three different people to ensure checks and balances. In the current Church structure, one person (the priest/bishop) holds all three roles. This leads to a lack of accountability.

3. Secularity of Ministry: An undue "sacredness" attached to priesthood while neglecting the wisdom of the laity.

4. Worldly Values: The world worships wealth, position, and pleasure, making it hard for Church leaders to live by opposite values.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Module 4: Praxis – How to Move Forward

4.1 Three Essential Shifts (The "Conversion")

To implement Synodality, individuals and institutions must undergo three shifts:

1. From Self-Enhancement Self-Emptying: (Kenosis).

2. From Self-Orientation Mission-Orientation: Focusing outward.

3. From Walking Alone Walking Together:.

4.2 Creating a "Pervasive Ethos" (Public Rationality)

Synodality must become the "Ethos" (the unwritten atmosphere) of society, just as democracy has become an ethos we take for granted.

Specifications for this Ethos:

• Disqualification for Ambition: Anyone aspiring for power for the sake of power should be immediately disqualified from leadership.

• Experience > Rubrics: We must move away from "excessive rubrics" (rigid rules). Uniformity should not kill the "mediated spiritual experience" of the people.

• Education: Synodality must be taught from kindergarten up to create a future generation where "walking together" is natural.

• Inter-religious Discernment: Using the principle of discernment found in the Quran, Bhagavad Gita, and Buddhist texts to aid public decision-making.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Module 5: Pilgrim’s Hope

5.1 Definition of Christian Hope

• It is not wishful thinking.

• Definition: Hope is an Agency and a real potential for action. It is the horizon that empowers us to act here and now.

5.2 Characteristics of this Hope

1. Unconquered Goal: The destination (Reign of God) spurs us on despite hardships.

2. Motivating Factor: It keeps the destination in our creative consciousness.

3. Communal Strength: It is strengthened by the presence of fellow pilgrims. The "heat of the neighbor" energizes us.

5.3 Conclusion

We are not the "owners" of the mission; the Holy Spirit is the master builder. We are merely workers. This realization frees us from anxiety about the results. We may not see the finished building, but we must lay the bricks today.
