From Ashes to Alleluia (Lenten Talk)

Overview

"FROM ASHES TO ALLELUIA: THE PRIEST TRANSFORMED"

A Three-Talk Lenten Retreat Series for Priests of West Bengal

Overall Arc: The three talks follow a single theological movement — we begin by acknowledging who we really are (humility), then enter the wilderness where God reshapes us (conversion), and finally emerge as men who can genuinely raise others to new life (mission). Each talk is the condition for the next. You cannot go to the desert if you don't know you are dust. You cannot raise Lazarus if you have never yourself been raised.

TALK ONE

"Remember, Priest, That You Are Dust"

On Holy Humility, Holy Hilarity, and the Shocking Grace of Being Ordinary

45 Minutes | Theme: Kenosis — Emptying as the Beginning of Everything

OPENING: THE LAUGH OF RECOGNITION (5 minutes)

Begin with this: There is a famous story — possibly apocryphal, certainly true — of a newly ordained priest who, full of fire and theological precision, preached his first homily for 55 minutes. After Mass, an elderly woman came up to him and said, "Father, you remind me of Jesus." He was thrilled. "Really? Why?" She said, "Because before you started, I had no idea how long this was going to last, and by the end, I just wanted to rise from the dead."

Brothers, we laugh — and we should — because there is something deeply recognizable in that story. We have all been that priest. Certain of our importance. Unaware of how heavy we have become.

Lent begins with ashes. Not incense. Not gold. Ashes. And the Church, with great theological audacity, walks up to the most educated men in the room — the men who just preached at others — and smears dirt on their foreheads and says: "Remember, man, that you are dust."

God does not begin Lent with a compliment. God begins Lent with a diagnosis.

I. THE BIBLICAL FOUNDATION: THE DUST THAT GOD LOVES (10 minutes)

The Hebrew word for the ground is adamah. The word for man is adam. We are, etymologically, dirt with delusions of grandeur. Genesis 2:7 says: "The Lord God formed the man from the dust of the ground and breathed into his nostrils the breath of life, and the man became a living being."

Notice the sequence. First dust. Then breath. The breath does not eliminate the dust — it animates it. The priest is not someone who has transcended his humanness. He is humanness that has been breathed upon. This is crucial because we spend enormous energy trying to hide the dust.

Then there is Psalm 8 — that magnificent oscillation of wonder: "What is man that you are mindful of him?" (Ps. 8:4) It is not a despairing question. It is an astonished one. The Psalmist is not saying "we are worthless" — he is saying "we are inexplicably, outrageously loved." There is a difference between humility and self-contempt, and priests often confuse the two.

St. Paul puts it with characteristic bluntness in 2 Corinthians 4:7 — one of the most important verses for understanding priesthood: "But we have this treasure in clay jars, so that it may be made clear that this extraordinary power belongs to God and does not come from us."

Ostrakina skeue — earthen vessels. Not silver. Not gold. Clay. The kind that chips. The kind that leaks. The kind that, if you drop it, breaks. Brothers, Paul is not apologizing for this. He is celebrating it. The very fragility of the vessel is what makes the light visible. A gold jar hides the flame. A cracked clay pot lets the light leak through the fractures.

Leonard Cohen — not a Catholic theologian, but occasionally a prophetic one — sang: "Ring the bells that still can ring / Forget your perfect offering / There is a crack in everything / That's how the light gets in."

That is Pauline theology set to music.

II. VATICAN II ON THE PRIEST AS HUMAN (8 minutes)

The Second Vatican Council, in Presbyterorum Ordinis (Decree on the Ministry and Life of Priests), made a statement that was, at the time, almost startling in its frankness. It said that priests are taken "from among men and appointed for men in things pertaining to God" (PO 3).

The Council did not say priests are taken from among the spiritually exceptional. It said: from among men. The raw material of priesthood is not holiness — it is humanity. God works with what is available, which is us.

Even more striking is what Gaudium et Spes 22 says — that Christ, by his incarnation, united himself "with every human being." Every. Not just the devout ones. Not just the ones who show up to daily Mass. Every. This means the humanness of the priest — including his struggles, his doubts, his bad days, his failed homilies, his occasional boredom during the Liturgy of the Hours — this is not an obstacle to priesthood. It is the medium through which priesthood operates.

Lumen Gentium 28 speaks of priests as those who make the bishop present in local communities. But the word used for this presence is repraesentant — they represent. They do not replace. There is always an excess. A surplus. Something in priestly ministry that the priest himself cannot account for, control, or take credit for. This is deeply liberating, brothers. You are not the show. You are the instrument.

III. THE POPES SPEAK: FRANCIS, BENEDICT, AND THE COURAGE OF SMALLNESS (8 minutes)

Pope Francis, in Evangelii Gaudium 94, delivered what I think is one of the most honest diagnoses of clerical dysfunction ever written by a Pope. He speaks of priests who suffer from "a tomb psychology" — men who have placed their hearts in a tomb and pulled the stone over themselves. He is not unkind about it. He is, however, mercilessly accurate. And then — this is the Francis move — he says the solution is not more effort. It is encounter. "I invite all Christians, everywhere, at this very moment, to a renewed personal encounter with Jesus Christ" (EG 3).

Benedict XVI, in his homily for the Chrism Mass of 2012, said something I find almost unbearably beautiful: "The priest is not alone. He is not acting on his own initiative; he represents Another. He places his words, his 'I', at the service of Another, of the One who sent him."

Brothers, read that again. You place your I at the service of Another. This is precisely what you did at ordination. You did not become more yourself. You became a window. The trick is not to let the window get so smudged with ego that no one can see through it.

John Paul II, in Pastores Dabo Vobis 43, spoke of the need for priests to cultivate "human formation" as the foundation of all other formation. He said — and this is often forgotten — that "the priest should mold his human personality in such a way that it becomes a bridge and not an obstacle for others in their meeting with Jesus Christ."

A bridge. Not a blockade. Not a toll booth. A bridge.

Here in West Bengal, where you move between cultures, between languages, between the fishermen of the Sundarbans and the professors of Jadavpur — this bridging vocation is not metaphorical. It is daily. And it begins with the humility to know that the bridge is not the destination. The other shore is.

IV. THE THEOLOGIANS: NOUWEN, RAHNER, AND THE WOUNDED HEALER (8 minutes)

Henri Nouwen — the Dutch priest and psychologist who ended his career not in a prestigious university but washing the clothes of adults with developmental disabilities at L'Arche — wrote a book called The Wounded Healer that every priest should read twice: once when ordained, once after the first pastoral crisis. Nouwen's central claim is audacious: "The minister is called to recognize the sufferings of his time in his own heart and make that recognition the starting point of his service."

Not your theological education. Not your organizational competence. Your wounds. Your recognizable, relatable, human wounds. The priest who has never struggled to pray cannot help a parishioner who has stopped praying. The priest who has never doubted cannot accompany someone in their dark night.

Karl Rahner — who was not always easy to read, which may be why priests often assign him to seminarians as a form of hazing — said something remarkably simple beneath all his conceptual complexity: "The devout Christian of the future will either be a mystic... or will cease to be anything at all." (Concern for the Church, 1981). He was writing about the laity, but it applies with even more urgency to priests. The priest who has not learned to descend into his own interior life — who has not made the inward journey — will eventually have nothing to give outward.

Romano Guardini, the Italian-German theologian whom Benedict XVI wrote his second doctoral thesis on, spoke of the Christian life as a perpetual movement between Gegensätze — polarities held in creative tension. The priest is simultaneously servant and leader, sinner and intercessor, weak and authoritative. The temptation is to collapse one pole into the other — to become only the authority, or to become so self-deprecating that you exercise no authority at all. Lent invites us to stand in the tension. Ashes and resurrection. Dust and breath. Human and holy.

V. PRACTICAL AND LOCAL REFLECTION: THE BENGALI PRIEST (4 minutes)

Rabindranath Tagore — who was not Christian but who understood the soul better than many who are — wrote in Gitanjali: "Where the mind is without fear and the head is held high..." But there is another line, less famous: "Where tireless striving stretches its arms towards perfection..." (Gitanjali 35).

The priest in Bengal strives. You serve communities where Catholicism is a minority within a minority. Where your flock may be Adivasi, or Bengali, or Anglo-Indian, or tribal, or urban. You preach in Bengali and English and sometimes Santali and sometimes just in gesture and presence. You baptize in places where there is no running water. You celebrate Eucharist in chapels that flood in June.

This is not incidental to your priesthood. This is your priesthood. And it begins — it must begin — with the willingness to be small enough, humble enough, to let God be the one who is large.

CLOSING OF TALK ONE: THE INVITATION (2 minutes)

The question to carry into silence after this talk is not "How can I become a better priest?" That question, while not wrong, is slightly off. The question is: "Am I willing to let God be the priest, and to be the clay jar?"

Because if you are willing — if you can receive the ashes with genuine interiority rather than liturgical routine — then you are ready for the desert. And that is where we go next.

"The Lord is near to the brokenhearted and saves the crushed in spirit." (Psalm 34:18)

TALK TWO

"Forty Days in the Desert: The Priest Who Dares to be Alone with God"

On Prayer, Silence, Spiritual Dryness, and Why God is Not Impressed by Our Busyness

45 Minutes | Theme: Metanoia — The Interior Conversion that Makes Exterior Ministry Possible

OPENING: THE PROBLEM OF THE BUSY PRIEST (5 minutes)

Brothers, let me describe a priest I know. He wakes at 5:30 AM for Morning Prayer — or rather, he intends to, but his phone buzzes with a WhatsApp message from a parishioner at 5:15, and by the time he puts it down, it is 6:45 and Mass begins in 15 minutes, so he makes the Morning Prayer into a kind of spiritual sprinting exercise. After Mass, there are meetings. Then a hospital visit. Then a school function where he must smile and say encouraging things to children whose names he cannot remember. Then lunch — eaten while answering emails. Then formation class. Then evening prayer — abbreviated, because the parish accounts need attention. Then dinner. Then he is exhausted.

He is doing everything. He is present nowhere.

This is not a caricature. Many of you recognized someone in that description. Some of you recognized yourselves. I am not here to make you feel guilty — guilt without grace is just self-punishment. I am here to say: this is precisely the desert Lent is calling you into. Not the desert of more tasks. The desert of radical interior availability.

Jesus went into the desert immediately after his baptism. Mark's Gospel — never one for delay — uses the Greek word euthus: immediately. The Spirit drives him out (Mark 1:12). This is not a gentle suggestion. The Spirit drives him. Perhaps because Jesus, left to his own devices as a young man full of divine energy and human enthusiasm, might have started healing people right away. But first: forty days. First: hunger. First: temptation. First: the long, terrible, beautiful silence.

I. THE BIBLICAL DESERT: NOT A PUNISHMENT BUT A SCHOOL (10 minutes)

The desert in the Hebrew imagination is not merely geographical. It is theological. Midbar — the Hebrew word for desert — shares a root with davar, meaning word. The desert is where God speaks, precisely because every other voice has been stripped away. Hosea 2:14 captures this with aching tenderness: "Therefore I will now allure her, and bring her into the wilderness, and speak tenderly to her."

God takes his people into the desert not to punish them but to court them. To get them alone. To speak without competition.

Moses spent forty years in the Midianite desert before he heard the burning bush. Elijah, burnt out and suicidal after the victory on Mount Carmel — because burnout is not a modern invention — ran into the desert, lay down under a broom tree, and said: "It is enough; now, O Lord, take away my life" (1 Kings 19:4). And God did not rebuke him. God put him to sleep. Then fed him. Then sent him back. Twice. "Get up and eat, for the journey is too much for you." (1 Kings 19:7).

Brothers, some of you are under that broom tree right now. This is not failure. This is the beginning of a conversation.

Jesus in the desert faces three temptations (Matthew 4:1-11), and they are not random. They are priestly temptations. Turn stones into bread — make ministry productive, make it tangible, make it quantifiable. Throw yourself from the pinnacle — perform, be spectacular, make people gasp. Accept all the kingdoms — acquire influence, accept the world's terms for success. These are the temptations that come for every priest, usually around year seven. Jesus refuses all three. With Scripture. With silence. With a radical rootedness in who he is and whose he is.

The desert does not create identity. It reveals it.

II. VATICAN II ON THE INTERIOR LIFE OF THE PRIEST (8 minutes)

Presbyterorum Ordinis 14 makes a claim that sounds simple but is actually revolutionary: "Priests will acquire holiness in their own proper way by exercising their functions sincerely and tirelessly in the Spirit of Christ."

Sincerely. Not efficiently. Not prolifically. Sincerely. The Latin is sincere — purely, without mixture of motive. PO 14 is saying that the pastoral activities themselves — if approached with genuine interiority — are the path to holiness. This is deeply consoling: you do not need to become a contemplative monk to grow in holiness. But — and this is the equally important counter-weight — it only works if the interiority is real. An exterior activity done without interior engagement produces activity, not holiness.

Optatam Totius 8 speaks of seminarians needing to learn "a certain interior freedom" — a concept that sounds vague until you have tried to lead a community meeting without it, or to sit at a deathbed without it, or to preach without it. Interior freedom is the ability to act without compulsion — without the need for approval, without fear of failure, without the anxious calculation of consequence. It is the fruit of prayer.

Gaudium et Spes 19 notes — with some sadness — that believers sometimes bear responsibility for atheism because they "neglect their own training in the faith, or teach erroneous doctrine, or are deficient in their religious, moral, or social life." The Council is not primarily blaming doctrine here. It is blaming witness. The priest who is clearly not praying is an argument against prayer. The priest who is visibly exhausted by ministry is an argument against vocation. The priest who is anxious is, implicitly, an argument against a God who is trustworthy.

III. THE POPES ON PRAYER: URGENT VOICES (8 minutes)

Pope Francis, in his morning meditation of November 9, 2013, said something that surprised his audience by its stark simplicity: "A bishop, a priest, who does not pray is dangerous." He was not being melodramatic. He went on: "He uses power as a tool of domination, not of service." Because without prayer — without the constant re-centering on God rather than self — the ministry gradually becomes about the minister.

In Laudato Si' — yes, the ecology encyclical — Francis includes a line that has nothing to do with ecology and everything to do with interior life: "We need to slow down and recover a sense of the real." (LS 225). Priests are losing the sense of the real. Not theological reality — they have that. The real: the face in front of them. The grief in the pew. The specific, unrepeatable person who has come to confession. This recovery of the real is what prayer does. Prayer re-humanizes the minister.

Benedict XVI, in Deus Caritas Est 7, wrote about the encounter with God in prayer in terms that should shake us: "Being Christian is not the result of an ethical choice or a lofty idea, but the encounter with an event, a person, which gives life a new horizon and a decisive direction." Notice: not an ethical choice. Not a lofty idea. An encounter. Benedict is telling us that priesthood which is not rooted in ongoing encounter with the living Christ degenerates. It becomes — his word, in Spe Salvi — mere "activism".

John Paul II, in Novo Millennio Ineunte 33, issued what amounts to a pastoral emergency order: "Before making practical plans, we need to promote a spirituality of communion." And communion — he clarifies — begins in contemplation. In gazing at the face of Christ. This is not optional spiritual enrichment for the especially devout. JPII calls it the "the high standard of ordinary Christian living" (NMI 31). Ordinary. The baseline. Not the peak.

IV. THE THEOLOGIANS: WHAT THE MYSTICS KNOW THAT WE HAVE FORGOTTEN (8 minutes)

Thomas Merton — the Trappist monk whose restless, searching, brilliant, infuriating, luminous life is itself a theology — wrote in New Seeds of Contemplation: "To say that I am made in the image of God is to say that love is the reason for my existence, for God is love. Love is my true identity. Selflessness is my true self. Love is my true character."

The desert — for Merton — is the place where you find out that all the identities you have constructed (the efficient administrator, the gifted preacher, the busy parish priest) are not you. The desert strips them. And what remains, if you can bear the stripping, is what was always there: the beloved of God.

Raniero Cantalamessa — the papal preacher, a Franciscan of extraordinary gifts — has said repeatedly in his Lenten sermons for the papal household that the great crisis of contemporary priesthood is not intellectual doubt, not theological error, but spiritual mediocrity. "The devil's greatest achievement," he writes in Sober Intoxication of the Spirit, "is not to make us into criminals but to make us into sleepwalkers." Sleepwalking priests. Going through the motions. Celebrating the sacraments. Giving the homilies. Present in body, absent in spirit.

Hans Urs von Balthasar — who was, with Cantalamessa, perhaps the most theologically sophisticated writer of the 20th century — wrote in Prayer that all authentic Christian action flows from contemplation, not as a bonus but as a necessity: "To be fertile in action, the apostle must be prepared to wait, in contemplation, for the hour of God." Wait for the hour of God. Brothers, when did you last wait? When did you last sit in silence long enough to hear something other than your own thoughts?

And here is where I must say something uncomfortable: the digital age has made genuine silence almost impossible, and many priests have — without deciding to — traded the desert for the scroll. WhatsApp. YouTube. Instagram reels of dubious spiritual value. Not because we are wicked, but because silence is genuinely difficult, and the scroll is genuinely easy. The desert demands we put down the phone. Not forever. Forty days.

V. THE LOCAL VOICE: MOTHER TERESA AND THE SILENCE OF CALCUTTA (4 minutes)

You are priests in West Bengal. You cannot speak about contemplation and silence without speaking of the woman from Skopje who became the soul of Kolkata. Mother Teresa — now Saint Teresa of Calcutta — who served in streets thirty minutes from where some of you minister — was not a mystic who happened to do social work. She was a contemplative whose social work was the overflow of her mysticism.

She said: "The fruit of silence is prayer. The fruit of prayer is faith. The fruit of faith is love. The fruit of love is service. The fruit of service is peace." This is a theological chain. Remove silence from the beginning, and the chain breaks. You may still have activity at the end — but it will not be peace-bearing. It will be exhausting and eventually embittering.

And — in a detail that humbles me every time I think about it — Mother Teresa went through nearly fifty years of spiritual darkness. The letters compiled in Come Be My Light reveal a woman who felt nothing, who heard nothing, who experienced God's absence as a physical wound — and who never stopped showing up. The darkness did not make her quit. It made her more compassionate. Because she knew, from the inside, what her poorest people felt.

Brothers, your spiritual dryness in prayer is not a disqualification. It is a qualification. It is the desert doing its work.

CLOSING OF TALK TWO: THE BRIDGE (2 minutes)

We have moved from dust (who we are) to desert (where we must go). The natural question is: then what? If I am dust, and if I have been through the desert, and if I have been stripped and re-encountered and re-centered — then what am I supposed to do with that?

That is the third talk. But before we get there, I want to leave you with this question to carry into the next session: Have I let God love me recently? Not have you served God. Have you let God love you. Because the priest who has been loved can love. The priest who has only been doing can only keep doing, until the doing breaks him.

"Come to me, all you who are weary and burdened, and I will give you rest." (Matthew 11:28)

The One who says that is waiting. The desert is not empty. It is full of Him.

TALK THREE

"Lazarus, Come Forth!": The Priest Who Raises the Dead"

On Mission, Mercy, Encounter, and Why the Church in Bengal Needs Men Who Have Been to the Desert

45 Minutes | Theme: Missio — The Transformation That Overflows into Others

OPENING: FROM CAVE TO STREET (5 minutes)

The story of Lazarus (John 11) is, on one level, about a dead man. On another level — the level I want to explore today — it is about a priest's vocation in precise, dramatic miniature.

Notice the sequence. Jesus does not rush. He waits two days after hearing Lazarus is ill (John 11:6). His disciples are baffled. Mary and Martha are grieving and slightly accusatory ("Lord, if you had been here..."). And then Jesus — who has spent time in prayer, who knows the Father's timing, who is not driven by crowd anxiety — arrives. Goes to the tomb. Weeps. Wept — the shortest verse in the Bible (John 11:35), and one of the most important for priestly spirituality: the Lord wept. Not as performance. Not as technique. He wept because he loved them.

And then he speaks. "Lazarus, come forth!"

And Lazarus comes. Wrapped in burial cloths, disoriented, blinking in the light. And Jesus says one more thing: "Unbind him, and let him go." (John 11:44)

Brothers, the priest is simultaneously all three figures in this story. He is Lazarus — someone who has been raised from his own smallness, his own death, by an encounter with Christ. He is the community — standing at the tomb, uncertain, hopeful, half-believing. And — by grace, not by his own power — he is called to speak the word that unbinds.

I. THE BIBLICAL MANDATE: YOU ARE SENT (10 minutes)

After the Resurrection, Jesus appears to the disciples in a locked room — they are afraid, as we all are when things have collapsed — and says: "As the Father has sent me, even so I send you." (John 20:21). The word is apostello — the root of apostle. Sent ones. Not invited ones. Not volunteers. Sent.

The sending follows the breathing: "He breathed on them and said, 'Receive the Holy Spirit.'" (John 20:22). Do you see the Genesis echo? He breathes. The Spirit animates the dust. And the animated dust goes out.

Luke 4:18 — Jesus' inaugural sermon, which he deliberately reads in the synagogue in Nazareth — is the priestly mission statement in Scripture: "The Spirit of the Lord is upon me, because he has anointed me to proclaim good news to the poor. He has sent me to proclaim liberty to the captives and recovering of sight to the blind, to set at liberty those who are oppressed, to proclaim the year of the Lord's favor."

Notice that this is a list of specific people. Not a general humanity. The poor. The captive. The blind. The oppressed. In West Bengal, these are not abstractions. These are your parishioners. The bonded laborers in the tea estates of North Bengal. The Adivasi Catholics of Jharkhand whose families migrated to the Sundarbans. The domestic workers in Kolkata who come to Sunday Mass with calloused hands and extraordinary faith. The fishermen of Diamond Harbour who trust a God they cannot see because they have learned to read water and weather and miracle.

Jesus announces the Year of Jubilee — the year of release, of debt-forgiveness, of land-return, of the restoration of dignity. The priest who has been through the desert, who has received his own freedom, now goes to announce freedom to others. Not with programs. With presence.

2 Corinthians 5:20 completes the picture: "We are ambassadors for Christ, God making his appeal through us." Ambassadors. Not diplomats in the sense of hedging and calculating — but representatives. When an ambassador speaks, the nation speaks. When the priest speaks at the bedside of the dying, or at the wedding of the uncertain, or at the baptism of the unexpected, Christ speaks. This is the awe-ful, wonderful, terrifying thing about ordination.

II. VATICAN II ON MISSION: THE CHURCH THAT GOES OUT (8 minutes)

Ad Gentes — the Council's Decree on Mission — opens with a phrase that should be tattooed on every seminary wall: "The Church on earth is by its very nature missionary, since, according to the plan of the Father, it has its origin in the mission of the Son and the Holy Spirit." (AG 2). Missionary by nature. Not by program. Not by budget line. By nature.

This means a Church that is not going out has stopped being fully itself. And a priest who has stopped going out — who has retreated into the sacristy of institutional maintenance — has quietly amputated part of his priesthood.

Gaudium et Spes 1 begins with a sentence that Pope Francis quotes so frequently it has almost become his personal motto: "The joys and hopes, the griefs and the anxieties of the people of this age, especially those who are poor or in any way afflicted — these are the joys and hopes, the griefs and anxieties of the followers of Christ."

The word for anxieties is aerumnae — sorrows, troubles, hardships. The Council is saying: your pastoral antennae must be tuned to joy and to grief in equal measure. The priest who cannot celebrate cannot accompany joy. The priest who cannot weep cannot accompany grief. And the priest who has not wept in the desert — who has not, like Jesus at the tomb of Lazarus, allowed real love to produce real tears — will remain, at the crucial moment, professional rather than human.

Lumen Gentium 10 distinguishes the common priesthood of the baptized from the ministerial priesthood, but crucially notes that they are "ordered one to another." The priest's role is not to do everything for the laity — it is to empower the laity to be the leaven. Particularly in Bengal, where the Catholic community is small, the priest who creates dependency rather than discipleship is misunderstanding his own role.

III. THE POPES ON MERCY AND MISSION (8 minutes)

Pope Francis opened the Extraordinary Jubilee of Mercy with a phrase that stopped many people cold: "The Church is not a tollhouse; it is the house of the Father." (Evangelii Gaudium 47). The contrast is precise and devastating. A tollhouse demands payment before entry. The Father's house — as the parable of the Prodigal Son shows — runs toward the returning son before he has spoken a word of apology.

In Misericordiae Vultus — the Bull of Indiction for the Jubilee Year — Francis wrote: "Mercy is the very foundation of the Church's life... Everything of her pastoral work should be caught up in the tenderness she makes present to believers." (MV 10). Tenderness. Not efficiency. Not correctness. Tenderness.

This does not mean softness about truth. Francis has been misread this way. It means that truth must be delivered with the hands of a father, not the clipboard of an inspector. The priest in the confessional who treats penitents as cases to be processed rather than persons to be encountered has — however orthodox his theology — missed the Jubilee entirely.

Benedict XVI, in Deus Caritas Est 31, wrote of Caritas workers — and by extension all pastoral workers — that they must be "men and women moved by Christ's love." Not men and women trained in technique. Moved. There is a passivity in that word. You cannot generate this movement. You receive it. In prayer. In the Eucharist. In the desert. And then it moves you outward.

John Paul II, addressing young people at World Youth Day 2002 in Toronto, said: "We are not the sum of our weaknesses and failures; we are the sum of the Father's love for us and our real capacity to become the image of his Son." Brothers, this applies to you. You are not the sum of your pastoral failures, your administrative disasters, your unfaithful parishioners, your leaking church roof, or your impossible bishop. You are the sum of the Father's love for you. And that love — if you receive it — makes you dangerous in the best possible way.

IV. THE THEOLOGIANS: ON THE OUTWARD MOVEMENT OF LOVE (8 minutes)

Karl Rahner, in The Shape of the Church to Come (1974), made a prediction that was not entirely optimistic but was entirely accurate: the Church of the future would be a Church of small communities — or it would not exist. In Bengal, you have been living this prophecy for generations. The Basic Christian Communities scattered across dioceses like Baruipur, Krishnagar, and Dinajpur — these are not a pastoral strategy. They are, in Rahner's sense, the future arriving early.

What Rahner also said — and this is the part we forget — is that these communities require leaders who are themselves mystics. Not administrators. Not even preachers primarily. Mystics: people who have encountered the living God and cannot shut up about it, because they have been sent by that encounter.

Hans Urs von Balthasar wrote in The Christian and Anxiety that authentic Christian mission is always characterized by a certain boldness that cannot be manufactured by technique or will power — it comes from the certainty, rooted in prayer and in the desert, that the One who sends is greater than every obstacle. "The apostle goes forth not because he is brave but because he has seen."

Yves Congar — the Dominican theologian who was silenced by Rome in the 1950s and then rehabilitated and made a Cardinal just before his death, in the kind of irony that God clearly enjoys — wrote in I Believe in the Holy Spirit that mission is always the Spirit's initiative. "We do not send ourselves. We are sent. And the Sender is present in the sending." The Sender is present. This is the guarantee. Not our competence. Not our charisma. The Sender.

And here — for priests in Bengal specifically — I must mention someone: Blessed Cyprian Michael Iwene Tansi, not Bengali, but an African priest whose life speaks directly to you: a man who found himself ministering in a minority community, surrounded by traditional religious practice, with limited resources and enormous cultural complexity. He chose not to condemn but to encounter. Not to impose but to propose. His path to sanctity ran directly through the humus of ordinary parish life. He is not a canonized saint yet in the universal sense — but he is beatified, and his life is an argument that holiness is available in the trenches.

Closer to home: Blessed Dom Isidore Bakanja, a Congolese lay catechist, beatified by JPII, who said before dying from wounds inflicted for his faith: "I pardon Reynders readily; he did not know what he was doing... Tell him that I want him to convert." This is mission completed — in the dying man's prayer for his killer. In Bengal, where the memory of Father Constant Lievens and the mass conversions of the Oraon and Munda people still breathes through your church history — mission has always involved this cost.

V. THE SPECIFIC CALL: THE PRIEST IN WEST BENGAL TODAY (4 minutes)

Brothers, you minister in a state that gave the world Rabindranath Tagore, Mother Teresa, and the left movement — simultaneously. A state where Durga Puja is a cultural phenomenon that even your Catholic parishioners celebrate with ambivalence and joy. A state where syncretic pressures are real, where interfaith dialogue is not an academic exercise but a Tuesday morning reality.

The priest who has been through the ashes of Talk One — who knows his clay-jar humanness — can sit with a Hindu neighbor without anxiety. He does not need to defend God. He needs to witness to encounter.

The priest who has been through the desert of Talk Two — who has acquired some interior stillness — can be present in the noise of a Bengali market, or a tribal festival, or a political rally affecting his parishioners, with a kind of holy groundedness that people recognize even when they cannot name it.

And the priest who stands at the tomb and says "Come forth!" — he is not commanding from above. He is standing alongside, having himself come forth. Having himself been unbound. And his word carries authority not because he is important but because he has been sent by One who is.

"I am the resurrection and the life. Whoever believes in me, though he die, yet shall he live." (John 11:25)

This is what you proclaim. Not an idea. A person. Not a program. An encounter. Not a system. A resurrection.

CLOSING: THE EASTER LOGIC (2 minutes)

Lent does not end with ashes. It ends with the Vigil fire. The whole arc — from the dust of Talk One, through the desert of Talk Two, to the tomb of Talk Three — is Easter logic. Dying and rising. Emptying and filling. Descending and ascending.

The priest who has walked this arc during Lent walks it liturgically every year — and ideally, every day. Because the Eucharist is precisely this: the taking of ordinary elements — bread, wine, human words, human hands — and their transformation into the Body and Blood of the Lord.

You do not cause this transformation. But you preside at it. And — if you are honest — you are yourself caught up in it, each time, if you let yourself be.

Go, brothers. Be dust. Go to the desert. Come forth. Unbind. And let them go.

"The Spirit of the Lord is upon me, because he has anointed me to bring good news to the poor." (Luke 4:18)

APPENDIX: PRACTICAL NOTES FOR THE RETREAT DIRECTOR

Session Flow: Each talk should be followed by 15–20 minutes of guided silent reflection, then small group sharing (3-4 priests), then optional one-to-one spiritual direction. Build the silences in. Do not fill them.

For Humor: The opening of each talk is intentionally lighter. Priests who feel seen in the humor will be more open to the harder theological content that follows. Don't rush the laugh — let it land.

Local Resonance: References to Tagore, Mother Teresa, the Bengal mission history, and the specific geography (Sundarbans, tea estates, Kolkata) should be expanded or adapted by the presenter based on the specific diocese represented.

Musical Suggestion: For the close of each talk, consider a sung response from the Bengali Christian folk tradition (baul-influenced Christian music exists in Bengal) — it would root the universal theology in the particular soil where these priests serve.

Bibliography for Further Reading:

Henri Nouwen, The Wounded Healer

Raniero Cantalamessa, Sober Intoxication of the Spirit

Pope Francis, Evangelii Gaudium and Gaudium et Spes

Karl Rahner, The Shape of the Church to Come

Hans Urs von Balthasar, Prayer

Thomas Merton, New Seeds of Contemplation

Vatican II Documents: Presbyterorum Ordinis, Ad Gentes, Lumen Gentium

"He who calls you is faithful; he will surely do it." — 1 Thessalonians 5:24

"FROM ASHES TO ALLELUIA: THE PRIEST TRANSFORMED"

A Three-Talk Lenten Retreat Series for Priests of West Bengal

Overall Arc: The three talks follow a single theological movement — we begin by acknowledging who we really are (humility), then enter the wilderness where God reshapes us (conversion), and finally emerge as men who can genuinely raise others to new life (mission). Each talk is the condition for the next. You cannot go to the desert if you don't know you are dust. You cannot raise Lazarus if you have never yourself been raised.

TALK ONE

"Remember, Priest, That You Are Dust"

On Holy Humility, Holy Hilarity, and the Shocking Grace of Being Ordinary

45 Minutes | Theme: Kenosis — Emptying as the Beginning of Everything

OPENING: THE LAUGH OF RECOGNITION (5 minutes)

Begin with this: There is a famous story — possibly apocryphal, certainly true — of a newly ordained priest who, full of fire and theological precision, preached his first homily for 55 minutes. After Mass, an elderly woman came up to him and said, "Father, you remind me of Jesus." He was thrilled. "Really? Why?" She said, "Because before you started, I had no idea how long this was going to last, and by the end, I just wanted to rise from the dead."

Brothers, we laugh — and we should — because there is something deeply recognizable in that story. We have all been that priest. Certain of our importance. Unaware of how heavy we have become.

Lent begins with ashes. Not incense. Not gold. Ashes. And the Church, with great theological audacity, walks up to the most educated men in the room — the men who just preached at others — and smears dirt on their foreheads and says: "Remember, man, that you are dust."

God does not begin Lent with a compliment. God begins Lent with a diagnosis.

I. THE BIBLICAL FOUNDATION: THE DUST THAT GOD LOVES (10 minutes)

The Hebrew word for the ground is adamah. The word for man is adam. We are, etymologically, dirt with delusions of grandeur. Genesis 2:7 says: "The Lord God formed the man from the dust of the ground and breathed into his nostrils the breath of life, and the man became a living being."

Notice the sequence. First dust. Then breath. The breath does not eliminate the dust — it animates it. The priest is not someone who has transcended his humanness. He is humanness that has been breathed upon. This is crucial because we spend enormous energy trying to hide the dust.

Then there is Psalm 8 — that magnificent oscillation of wonder: "What is man that you are mindful of him?" (Ps. 8:4) It is not a despairing question. It is an astonished one. The Psalmist is not saying "we are worthless" — he is saying "we are inexplicably, outrageously loved." There is a difference between humility and self-contempt, and priests often confuse the two.

St. Paul puts it with characteristic bluntness in 2 Corinthians 4:7 — one of the most important verses for understanding priesthood: "But we have this treasure in clay jars, so that it may be made clear that this extraordinary power belongs to God and does not come from us."

Ostrakina skeue — earthen vessels. Not silver. Not gold. Clay. The kind that chips. The kind that leaks. The kind that, if you drop it, breaks. Brothers, Paul is not apologizing for this. He is celebrating it. The very fragility of the vessel is what makes the light visible. A gold jar hides the flame. A cracked clay pot lets the light leak through the fractures.

Leonard Cohen — not a Catholic theologian, but occasionally a prophetic one — sang: "Ring the bells that still can ring / Forget your perfect offering / There is a crack in everything / That's how the light gets in."

That is Pauline theology set to music.

II. VATICAN II ON THE PRIEST AS HUMAN (8 minutes)

The Second Vatican Council, in Presbyterorum Ordinis (Decree on the Ministry and Life of Priests), made a statement that was, at the time, almost startling in its frankness. It said that priests are taken "from among men and appointed for men in things pertaining to God" (PO 3).

The Council did not say priests are taken from among the spiritually exceptional. It said: from among men. The raw material of priesthood is not holiness — it is humanity. God works with what is available, which is us.

Even more striking is what Gaudium et Spes 22 says — that Christ, by his incarnation, united himself "with every human being." Every. Not just the devout ones. Not just the ones who show up to daily Mass. Every. This means the humanness of the priest — including his struggles, his doubts, his bad days, his failed homilies, his occasional boredom during the Liturgy of the Hours — this is not an obstacle to priesthood. It is the medium through which priesthood operates.

Lumen Gentium 28 speaks of priests as those who make the bishop present in local communities. But the word used for this presence is repraesentant — they represent. They do not replace. There is always an excess. A surplus. Something in priestly ministry that the priest himself cannot account for, control, or take credit for. This is deeply liberating, brothers. You are not the show. You are the instrument.

III. THE POPES SPEAK: FRANCIS, BENEDICT, AND THE COURAGE OF SMALLNESS (8 minutes)

Pope Francis, in Evangelii Gaudium 94, delivered what I think is one of the most honest diagnoses of clerical dysfunction ever written by a Pope. He speaks of priests who suffer from "a tomb psychology" — men who have placed their hearts in a tomb and pulled the stone over themselves. He is not unkind about it. He is, however, mercilessly accurate. And then — this is the Francis move — he says the solution is not more effort. It is encounter. "I invite all Christians, everywhere, at this very moment, to a renewed personal encounter with Jesus Christ" (EG 3).

Benedict XVI, in his homily for the Chrism Mass of 2012, said something I find almost unbearably beautiful: "The priest is not alone. He is not acting on his own initiative; he represents Another. He places his words, his 'I', at the service of Another, of the One who sent him."

Brothers, read that again. You place your I at the service of Another. This is precisely what you did at ordination. You did not become more yourself. You became a window. The trick is not to let the window get so smudged with ego that no one can see through it.

John Paul II, in Pastores Dabo Vobis 43, spoke of the need for priests to cultivate "human formation" as the foundation of all other formation. He said — and this is often forgotten — that "the priest should mold his human personality in such a way that it becomes a bridge and not an obstacle for others in their meeting with Jesus Christ."

A bridge. Not a blockade. Not a toll booth. A bridge.

Here in West Bengal, where you move between cultures, between languages, between the fishermen of the Sundarbans and the professors of Jadavpur — this bridging vocation is not metaphorical. It is daily. And it begins with the humility to know that the bridge is not the destination. The other shore is.

IV. THE THEOLOGIANS: NOUWEN, RAHNER, AND THE WOUNDED HEALER (8 minutes)

Henri Nouwen — the Dutch priest and psychologist who ended his career not in a prestigious university but washing the clothes of adults with developmental disabilities at L'Arche — wrote a book called The Wounded Healer that every priest should read twice: once when ordained, once after the first pastoral crisis. Nouwen's central claim is audacious: "The minister is called to recognize the sufferings of his time in his own heart and make that recognition the starting point of his service."

Not your theological education. Not your organizational competence. Your wounds. Your recognizable, relatable, human wounds. The priest who has never struggled to pray cannot help a parishioner who has stopped praying. The priest who has never doubted cannot accompany someone in their dark night.

Karl Rahner — who was not always easy to read, which may be why priests often assign him to seminarians as a form of hazing — said something remarkably simple beneath all his conceptual complexity: "The devout Christian of the future will either be a mystic... or will cease to be anything at all." (Concern for the Church, 1981). He was writing about the laity, but it applies with even more urgency to priests. The priest who has not learned to descend into his own interior life — who has not made the inward journey — will eventually have nothing to give outward.

Romano Guardini, the Italian-German theologian whom Benedict XVI wrote his second doctoral thesis on, spoke of the Christian life as a perpetual movement between Gegensätze — polarities held in creative tension. The priest is simultaneously servant and leader, sinner and intercessor, weak and authoritative. The temptation is to collapse one pole into the other — to become only the authority, or to become so self-deprecating that you exercise no authority at all. Lent invites us to stand in the tension. Ashes and resurrection. Dust and breath. Human and holy.

V. PRACTICAL AND LOCAL REFLECTION: THE BENGALI PRIEST (4 minutes)

Rabindranath Tagore — who was not Christian but who understood the soul better than many who are — wrote in Gitanjali: "Where the mind is without fear and the head is held high..." But there is another line, less famous: "Where tireless striving stretches its arms towards perfection..." (Gitanjali 35).

The priest in Bengal strives. You serve communities where Catholicism is a minority within a minority. Where your flock may be Adivasi, or Bengali, or Anglo-Indian, or tribal, or urban. You preach in Bengali and English and sometimes Santali and sometimes just in gesture and presence. You baptize in places where there is no running water. You celebrate Eucharist in chapels that flood in June.

This is not incidental to your priesthood. This is your priesthood. And it begins — it must begin — with the willingness to be small enough, humble enough, to let God be the one who is large.

CLOSING OF TALK ONE: THE INVITATION (2 minutes)

The question to carry into silence after this talk is not "How can I become a better priest?" That question, while not wrong, is slightly off. The question is: "Am I willing to let God be the priest, and to be the clay jar?"

Because if you are willing — if you can receive the ashes with genuine interiority rather than liturgical routine — then you are ready for the desert. And that is where we go next.

"The Lord is near to the brokenhearted and saves the crushed in spirit." (Psalm 34:18)

TALK TWO

"Forty Days in the Desert: The Priest Who Dares to be Alone with God"

On Prayer, Silence, Spiritual Dryness, and Why God is Not Impressed by Our Busyness

45 Minutes | Theme: Metanoia — The Interior Conversion that Makes Exterior Ministry Possible

OPENING: THE PROBLEM OF THE BUSY PRIEST (5 minutes)

Brothers, let me describe a priest I know. He wakes at 5:30 AM for Morning Prayer — or rather, he intends to, but his phone buzzes with a WhatsApp message from a parishioner at 5:15, and by the time he puts it down, it is 6:45 and Mass begins in 15 minutes, so he makes the Morning Prayer into a kind of spiritual sprinting exercise. After Mass, there are meetings. Then a hospital visit. Then a school function where he must smile and say encouraging things to children whose names he cannot remember. Then lunch — eaten while answering emails. Then formation class. Then evening prayer — abbreviated, because the parish accounts need attention. Then dinner. Then he is exhausted.

He is doing everything. He is present nowhere.

This is not a caricature. Many of you recognized someone in that description. Some of you recognized yourselves. I am not here to make you feel guilty — guilt without grace is just self-punishment. I am here to say: this is precisely the desert Lent is calling you into. Not the desert of more tasks. The desert of radical interior availability.

Jesus went into the desert immediately after his baptism. Mark's Gospel — never one for delay — uses the Greek word euthus: immediately. The Spirit drives him out (Mark 1:12). This is not a gentle suggestion. The Spirit drives him. Perhaps because Jesus, left to his own devices as a young man full of divine energy and human enthusiasm, might have started healing people right away. But first: forty days. First: hunger. First: temptation. First: the long, terrible, beautiful silence.

I. THE BIBLICAL DESERT: NOT A PUNISHMENT BUT A SCHOOL (10 minutes)

The desert in the Hebrew imagination is not merely geographical. It is theological. Midbar — the Hebrew word for desert — shares a root with davar, meaning word. The desert is where God speaks, precisely because every other voice has been stripped away. Hosea 2:14 captures this with aching tenderness: "Therefore I will now allure her, and bring her into the wilderness, and speak tenderly to her."

God takes his people into the desert not to punish them but to court them. To get them alone. To speak without competition.

Moses spent forty years in the Midianite desert before he heard the burning bush. Elijah, burnt out and suicidal after the victory on Mount Carmel — because burnout is not a modern invention — ran into the desert, lay down under a broom tree, and said: "It is enough; now, O Lord, take away my life" (1 Kings 19:4). And God did not rebuke him. God put him to sleep. Then fed him. Then sent him back. Twice. "Get up and eat, for the journey is too much for you." (1 Kings 19:7).

Brothers, some of you are under that broom tree right now. This is not failure. This is the beginning of a conversation.

Jesus in the desert faces three temptations (Matthew 4:1-11), and they are not random. They are priestly temptations. Turn stones into bread — make ministry productive, make it tangible, make it quantifiable. Throw yourself from the pinnacle — perform, be spectacular, make people gasp. Accept all the kingdoms — acquire influence, accept the world's terms for success. These are the temptations that come for every priest, usually around year seven. Jesus refuses all three. With Scripture. With silence. With a radical rootedness in who he is and whose he is.

The desert does not create identity. It reveals it.

II. VATICAN II ON THE INTERIOR LIFE OF THE PRIEST (8 minutes)

Presbyterorum Ordinis 14 makes a claim that sounds simple but is actually revolutionary: "Priests will acquire holiness in their own proper way by exercising their functions sincerely and tirelessly in the Spirit of Christ."

Sincerely. Not efficiently. Not prolifically. Sincerely. The Latin is sincere — purely, without mixture of motive. PO 14 is saying that the pastoral activities themselves — if approached with genuine interiority — are the path to holiness. This is deeply consoling: you do not need to become a contemplative monk to grow in holiness. But — and this is the equally important counter-weight — it only works if the interiority is real. An exterior activity done without interior engagement produces activity, not holiness.

Optatam Totius 8 speaks of seminarians needing to learn "a certain interior freedom" — a concept that sounds vague until you have tried to lead a community meeting without it, or to sit at a deathbed without it, or to preach without it. Interior freedom is the ability to act without compulsion — without the need for approval, without fear of failure, without the anxious calculation of consequence. It is the fruit of prayer.

Gaudium et Spes 19 notes — with some sadness — that believers sometimes bear responsibility for atheism because they "neglect their own training in the faith, or teach erroneous doctrine, or are deficient in their religious, moral, or social life." The Council is not primarily blaming doctrine here. It is blaming witness. The priest who is clearly not praying is an argument against prayer. The priest who is visibly exhausted by ministry is an argument against vocation. The priest who is anxious is, implicitly, an argument against a God who is trustworthy.

III. THE POPES ON PRAYER: URGENT VOICES (8 minutes)

Pope Francis, in his morning meditation of November 9, 2013, said something that surprised his audience by its stark simplicity: "A bishop, a priest, who does not pray is dangerous." He was not being melodramatic. He went on: "He uses power as a tool of domination, not of service." Because without prayer — without the constant re-centering on God rather than self — the ministry gradually becomes about the minister.

In Laudato Si' — yes, the ecology encyclical — Francis includes a line that has nothing to do with ecology and everything to do with interior life: "We need to slow down and recover a sense of the real." (LS 225). Priests are losing the sense of the real. Not theological reality — they have that. The real: the face in front of them. The grief in the pew. The specific, unrepeatable person who has come to confession. This recovery of the real is what prayer does. Prayer re-humanizes the minister.

Benedict XVI, in Deus Caritas Est 7, wrote about the encounter with God in prayer in terms that should shake us: "Being Christian is not the result of an ethical choice or a lofty idea, but the encounter with an event, a person, which gives life a new horizon and a decisive direction." Notice: not an ethical choice. Not a lofty idea. An encounter. Benedict is telling us that priesthood which is not rooted in ongoing encounter with the living Christ degenerates. It becomes — his word, in Spe Salvi — mere "activism".

John Paul II, in Novo Millennio Ineunte 33, issued what amounts to a pastoral emergency order: "Before making practical plans, we need to promote a spirituality of communion." And communion — he clarifies — begins in contemplation. In gazing at the face of Christ. This is not optional spiritual enrichment for the especially devout. JPII calls it the "the high standard of ordinary Christian living" (NMI 31). Ordinary. The baseline. Not the peak.

IV. THE THEOLOGIANS: WHAT THE MYSTICS KNOW THAT WE HAVE FORGOTTEN (8 minutes)

Thomas Merton — the Trappist monk whose restless, searching, brilliant, infuriating, luminous life is itself a theology — wrote in New Seeds of Contemplation: "To say that I am made in the image of God is to say that love is the reason for my existence, for God is love. Love is my true identity. Selflessness is my true self. Love is my true character."

The desert — for Merton — is the place where you find out that all the identities you have constructed (the efficient administrator, the gifted preacher, the busy parish priest) are not you. The desert strips them. And what remains, if you can bear the stripping, is what was always there: the beloved of God.

Raniero Cantalamessa — the papal preacher, a Franciscan of extraordinary gifts — has said repeatedly in his Lenten sermons for the papal household that the great crisis of contemporary priesthood is not intellectual doubt, not theological error, but spiritual mediocrity. "The devil's greatest achievement," he writes in Sober Intoxication of the Spirit, "is not to make us into criminals but to make us into sleepwalkers." Sleepwalking priests. Going through the motions. Celebrating the sacraments. Giving the homilies. Present in body, absent in spirit.

Hans Urs von Balthasar — who was, with Cantalamessa, perhaps the most theologically sophisticated writer of the 20th century — wrote in Prayer that all authentic Christian action flows from contemplation, not as a bonus but as a necessity: "To be fertile in action, the apostle must be prepared to wait, in contemplation, for the hour of God." Wait for the hour of God. Brothers, when did you last wait? When did you last sit in silence long enough to hear something other than your own thoughts?

And here is where I must say something uncomfortable: the digital age has made genuine silence almost impossible, and many priests have — without deciding to — traded the desert for the scroll. WhatsApp. YouTube. Instagram reels of dubious spiritual value. Not because we are wicked, but because silence is genuinely difficult, and the scroll is genuinely easy. The desert demands we put down the phone. Not forever. Forty days.

V. THE LOCAL VOICE: MOTHER TERESA AND THE SILENCE OF CALCUTTA (4 minutes)

You are priests in West Bengal. You cannot speak about contemplation and silence without speaking of the woman from Skopje who became the soul of Kolkata. Mother Teresa — now Saint Teresa of Calcutta — who served in streets thirty minutes from where some of you minister — was not a mystic who happened to do social work. She was a contemplative whose social work was the overflow of her mysticism.

She said: "The fruit of silence is prayer. The fruit of prayer is faith. The fruit of faith is love. The fruit of love is service. The fruit of service is peace." This is a theological chain. Remove silence from the beginning, and the chain breaks. You may still have activity at the end — but it will not be peace-bearing. It will be exhausting and eventually embittering.

And — in a detail that humbles me every time I think about it — Mother Teresa went through nearly fifty years of spiritual darkness. The letters compiled in Come Be My Light reveal a woman who felt nothing, who heard nothing, who experienced God's absence as a physical wound — and who never stopped showing up. The darkness did not make her quit. It made her more compassionate. Because she knew, from the inside, what her poorest people felt.

Brothers, your spiritual dryness in prayer is not a disqualification. It is a qualification. It is the desert doing its work.

CLOSING OF TALK TWO: THE BRIDGE (2 minutes)

We have moved from dust (who we are) to desert (where we must go). The natural question is: then what? If I am dust, and if I have been through the desert, and if I have been stripped and re-encountered and re-centered — then what am I supposed to do with that?

That is the third talk. But before we get there, I want to leave you with this question to carry into the next session: Have I let God love me recently? Not have you served God. Have you let God love you. Because the priest who has been loved can love. The priest who has only been doing can only keep doing, until the doing breaks him.

"Come to me, all you who are weary and burdened, and I will give you rest." (Matthew 11:28)

The One who says that is waiting. The desert is not empty. It is full of Him.

TALK THREE

"Lazarus, Come Forth!": The Priest Who Raises the Dead"

On Mission, Mercy, Encounter, and Why the Church in Bengal Needs Men Who Have Been to the Desert

45 Minutes | Theme: Missio — The Transformation That Overflows into Others

OPENING: FROM CAVE TO STREET (5 minutes)

The story of Lazarus (John 11) is, on one level, about a dead man. On another level — the level I want to explore today — it is about a priest's vocation in precise, dramatic miniature.

Notice the sequence. Jesus does not rush. He waits two days after hearing Lazarus is ill (John 11:6). His disciples are baffled. Mary and Martha are grieving and slightly accusatory ("Lord, if you had been here..."). And then Jesus — who has spent time in prayer, who knows the Father's timing, who is not driven by crowd anxiety — arrives. Goes to the tomb. Weeps. Wept — the shortest verse in the Bible (John 11:35), and one of the most important for priestly spirituality: the Lord wept. Not as performance. Not as technique. He wept because he loved them.

And then he speaks. "Lazarus, come forth!"

And Lazarus comes. Wrapped in burial cloths, disoriented, blinking in the light. And Jesus says one more thing: "Unbind him, and let him go." (John 11:44)

Brothers, the priest is simultaneously all three figures in this story. He is Lazarus — someone who has been raised from his own smallness, his own death, by an encounter with Christ. He is the community — standing at the tomb, uncertain, hopeful, half-believing. And — by grace, not by his own power — he is called to speak the word that unbinds.

I. THE BIBLICAL MANDATE: YOU ARE SENT (10 minutes)

After the Resurrection, Jesus appears to the disciples in a locked room — they are afraid, as we all are when things have collapsed — and says: "As the Father has sent me, even so I send you." (John 20:21). The word is apostello — the root of apostle. Sent ones. Not invited ones. Not volunteers. Sent.

The sending follows the breathing: "He breathed on them and said, 'Receive the Holy Spirit.'" (John 20:22). Do you see the Genesis echo? He breathes. The Spirit animates the dust. And the animated dust goes out.

Luke 4:18 — Jesus' inaugural sermon, which he deliberately reads in the synagogue in Nazareth — is the priestly mission statement in Scripture: "The Spirit of the Lord is upon me, because he has anointed me to proclaim good news to the poor. He has sent me to proclaim liberty to the captives and recovering of sight to the blind, to set at liberty those who are oppressed, to proclaim the year of the Lord's favor."

Notice that this is a list of specific people. Not a general humanity. The poor. The captive. The blind. The oppressed. In West Bengal, these are not abstractions. These are your parishioners. The bonded laborers in the tea estates of North Bengal. The Adivasi Catholics of Jharkhand whose families migrated to the Sundarbans. The domestic workers in Kolkata who come to Sunday Mass with calloused hands and extraordinary faith. The fishermen of Diamond Harbour who trust a God they cannot see because they have learned to read water and weather and miracle.

Jesus announces the Year of Jubilee — the year of release, of debt-forgiveness, of land-return, of the restoration of dignity. The priest who has been through the desert, who has received his own freedom, now goes to announce freedom to others. Not with programs. With presence.

2 Corinthians 5:20 completes the picture: "We are ambassadors for Christ, God making his appeal through us." Ambassadors. Not diplomats in the sense of hedging and calculating — but representatives. When an ambassador speaks, the nation speaks. When the priest speaks at the bedside of the dying, or at the wedding of the uncertain, or at the baptism of the unexpected, Christ speaks. This is the awe-ful, wonderful, terrifying thing about ordination.

II. VATICAN II ON MISSION: THE CHURCH THAT GOES OUT (8 minutes)

Ad Gentes — the Council's Decree on Mission — opens with a phrase that should be tattooed on every seminary wall: "The Church on earth is by its very nature missionary, since, according to the plan of the Father, it has its origin in the mission of the Son and the Holy Spirit." (AG 2). Missionary by nature. Not by program. Not by budget line. By nature.

This means a Church that is not going out has stopped being fully itself. And a priest who has stopped going out — who has retreated into the sacristy of institutional maintenance — has quietly amputated part of his priesthood.

Gaudium et Spes 1 begins with a sentence that Pope Francis quotes so frequently it has almost become his personal motto: "The joys and hopes, the griefs and the anxieties of the people of this age, especially those who are poor or in any way afflicted — these are the joys and hopes, the griefs and anxieties of the followers of Christ."

The word for anxieties is aerumnae — sorrows, troubles, hardships. The Council is saying: your pastoral antennae must be tuned to joy and to grief in equal measure. The priest who cannot celebrate cannot accompany joy. The priest who cannot weep cannot accompany grief. And the priest who has not wept in the desert — who has not, like Jesus at the tomb of Lazarus, allowed real love to produce real tears — will remain, at the crucial moment, professional rather than human.

Lumen Gentium 10 distinguishes the common priesthood of the baptized from the ministerial priesthood, but crucially notes that they are "ordered one to another." The priest's role is not to do everything for the laity — it is to empower the laity to be the leaven. Particularly in Bengal, where the Catholic community is small, the priest who creates dependency rather than discipleship is misunderstanding his own role.

III. THE POPES ON MERCY AND MISSION (8 minutes)

Pope Francis opened the Extraordinary Jubilee of Mercy with a phrase that stopped many people cold: "The Church is not a tollhouse; it is the house of the Father." (Evangelii Gaudium 47). The contrast is precise and devastating. A tollhouse demands payment before entry. The Father's house — as the parable of the Prodigal Son shows — runs toward the returning son before he has spoken a word of apology.

In Misericordiae Vultus — the Bull of Indiction for the Jubilee Year — Francis wrote: "Mercy is the very foundation of the Church's life... Everything of her pastoral work should be caught up in the tenderness she makes present to believers." (MV 10). Tenderness. Not efficiency. Not correctness. Tenderness.

This does not mean softness about truth. Francis has been misread this way. It means that truth must be delivered with the hands of a father, not the clipboard of an inspector. The priest in the confessional who treats penitents as cases to be processed rather than persons to be encountered has — however orthodox his theology — missed the Jubilee entirely.

Benedict XVI, in Deus Caritas Est 31, wrote of Caritas workers — and by extension all pastoral workers — that they must be "men and women moved by Christ's love." Not men and women trained in technique. Moved. There is a passivity in that word. You cannot generate this movement. You receive it. In prayer. In the Eucharist. In the desert. And then it moves you outward.

John Paul II, addressing young people at World Youth Day 2002 in Toronto, said: "We are not the sum of our weaknesses and failures; we are the sum of the Father's love for us and our real capacity to become the image of his Son." Brothers, this applies to you. You are not the sum of your pastoral failures, your administrative disasters, your unfaithful parishioners, your leaking church roof, or your impossible bishop. You are the sum of the Father's love for you. And that love — if you receive it — makes you dangerous in the best possible way.

IV. THE THEOLOGIANS: ON THE OUTWARD MOVEMENT OF LOVE (8 minutes)

Karl Rahner, in The Shape of the Church to Come (1974), made a prediction that was not entirely optimistic but was entirely accurate: the Church of the future would be a Church of small communities — or it would not exist. In Bengal, you have been living this prophecy for generations. The Basic Christian Communities scattered across dioceses like Baruipur, Krishnagar, and Dinajpur — these are not a pastoral strategy. They are, in Rahner's sense, the future arriving early.

What Rahner also said — and this is the part we forget — is that these communities require leaders who are themselves mystics. Not administrators. Not even preachers primarily. Mystics: people who have encountered the living God and cannot shut up about it, because they have been sent by that encounter.

Hans Urs von Balthasar wrote in The Christian and Anxiety that authentic Christian mission is always characterized by a certain boldness that cannot be manufactured by technique or will power — it comes from the certainty, rooted in prayer and in the desert, that the One who sends is greater than every obstacle. "The apostle goes forth not because he is brave but because he has seen."

Yves Congar — the Dominican theologian who was silenced by Rome in the 1950s and then rehabilitated and made a Cardinal just before his death, in the kind of irony that God clearly enjoys — wrote in I Believe in the Holy Spirit that mission is always the Spirit's initiative. "We do not send ourselves. We are sent. And the Sender is present in the sending." The Sender is present. This is the guarantee. Not our competence. Not our charisma. The Sender.

And here — for priests in Bengal specifically — I must mention someone: Blessed Cyprian Michael Iwene Tansi, not Bengali, but an African priest whose life speaks directly to you: a man who found himself ministering in a minority community, surrounded by traditional religious practice, with limited resources and enormous cultural complexity. He chose not to condemn but to encounter. Not to impose but to propose. His path to sanctity ran directly through the humus of ordinary parish life. He is not a canonized saint yet in the universal sense — but he is beatified, and his life is an argument that holiness is available in the trenches.

Closer to home: Blessed Dom Isidore Bakanja, a Congolese lay catechist, beatified by JPII, who said before dying from wounds inflicted for his faith: "I pardon Reynders readily; he did not know what he was doing... Tell him that I want him to convert." This is mission completed — in the dying man's prayer for his killer. In Bengal, where the memory of Father Constant Lievens and the mass conversions of the Oraon and Munda people still breathes through your church history — mission has always involved this cost.

V. THE SPECIFIC CALL: THE PRIEST IN WEST BENGAL TODAY (4 minutes)

Brothers, you minister in a state that gave the world Rabindranath Tagore, Mother Teresa, and the left movement — simultaneously. A state where Durga Puja is a cultural phenomenon that even your Catholic parishioners celebrate with ambivalence and joy. A state where syncretic pressures are real, where interfaith dialogue is not an academic exercise but a Tuesday morning reality.

The priest who has been through the ashes of Talk One — who knows his clay-jar humanness — can sit with a Hindu neighbor without anxiety. He does not need to defend God. He needs to witness to encounter.

The priest who has been through the desert of Talk Two — who has acquired some interior stillness — can be present in the noise of a Bengali market, or a tribal festival, or a political rally affecting his parishioners, with a kind of holy groundedness that people recognize even when they cannot name it.

And the priest who stands at the tomb and says "Come forth!" — he is not commanding from above. He is standing alongside, having himself come forth. Having himself been unbound. And his word carries authority not because he is important but because he has been sent by One who is.

"I am the resurrection and the life. Whoever believes in me, though he die, yet shall he live." (John 11:25)

This is what you proclaim. Not an idea. A person. Not a program. An encounter. Not a system. A resurrection.

CLOSING: THE EASTER LOGIC (2 minutes)

Lent does not end with ashes. It ends with the Vigil fire. The whole arc — from the dust of Talk One, through the desert of Talk Two, to the tomb of Talk Three — is Easter logic. Dying and rising. Emptying and filling. Descending and ascending.

The priest who has walked this arc during Lent walks it liturgically every year — and ideally, every day. Because the Eucharist is precisely this: the taking of ordinary elements — bread, wine, human words, human hands — and their transformation into the Body and Blood of the Lord.

You do not cause this transformation. But you preside at it. And — if you are honest — you are yourself caught up in it, each time, if you let yourself be.

Go, brothers. Be dust. Go to the desert. Come forth. Unbind. And let them go.

"The Spirit of the Lord is upon me, because he has anointed me to bring good news to the poor." (Luke 4:18)

APPENDIX: PRACTICAL NOTES FOR THE RETREAT DIRECTOR

Session Flow: Each talk should be followed by 15–20 minutes of guided silent reflection, then small group sharing (3-4 priests), then optional one-to-one spiritual direction. Build the silences in. Do not fill them.

For Humor: The opening of each talk is intentionally lighter. Priests who feel seen in the humor will be more open to the harder theological content that follows. Don't rush the laugh — let it land.

Local Resonance: References to Tagore, Mother Teresa, the Bengal mission history, and the specific geography (Sundarbans, tea estates, Kolkata) should be expanded or adapted by the presenter based on the specific diocese represented.

Musical Suggestion: For the close of each talk, consider a sung response from the Bengali Christian folk tradition (baul-influenced Christian music exists in Bengal) — it would root the universal theology in the particular soil where these priests serve.

Bibliography for Further Reading:

Henri Nouwen, The Wounded Healer

Raniero Cantalamessa, Sober Intoxication of the Spirit

Pope Francis, Evangelii Gaudium and Gaudium et Spes

Karl Rahner, The Shape of the Church to Come

Hans Urs von Balthasar, Prayer

Thomas Merton, New Seeds of Contemplation

Vatican II Documents: Presbyterorum Ordinis, Ad Gentes, Lumen Gentium

"He who calls you is faithful; he will surely do it." — 1 Thessalonians 5:24
