Talk 1 and 2 MSC

Overview

KING DAVID: A MAN AFTER GOD’S OWN HEART

A Biblical Recollection for Theologians

Why King David Matters for Theology Students

Among all biblical characters, David holds a unique place. God Himself declares: “I have found David the son of Jesse, a man after my own heart, who will do all I want him to do” (Acts 13:22).

This is not human praise—this is God's own testimony.

Four Reasons to Study David

1. Divine Commendation No other biblical figure receives this exact description from God: “a man after my own heart.”

2. Biblical Prominence David is mentioned in Scripture more than any other biblical character, signaling his importance for our learning.

3. Messianic Significance Even the blind beggar recognized Jesus not as “Son of Mary” but as “Son of David” (Mark 10:47)—David's lineage points directly to Christ.

4. Complete Theological Portrait David's life reveals both the heights and depths of human experience with God:

Chosen while forgotten by his own family

Courageous against impossible odds

Faithful through 13 years of persecution

Sinful in adultery and murder

Repentant and immediately restored

Used by God despite his failures

INTRODUCTION: David as GOD’S CHOSEN KING

God’s Declaration: “I chose David to be the king of My People Israel” (1 Kings 8:16)

David’s Biblical Prominence:

David is mentioned in the Bible more times than any other Biblical character

David’s legacy extends beyond his lifetime—God preserves one tribe for Solomon “for the sake of my servant David”

God’s covenant with David endures: “so that my servant David may always have a lamp before me in Jerusalem” (1 Kings 11:36)

The Contrast with Solomon: When God decided to tear the kingdom from Solomon’s hand, He declared: “Solomon has not walked in my ways to do what is right before me. I will let Solomon reign during his lifetime for the sake of David my servant whom I chose” (1 Kings 11:30-36). On tribe will be left for Solomon’s son, so that one lamp will be before God in Jerusalem

DAVID’S HUMILITY BEFORE GOD

David’s Prayer of wonder and Astonishment (2 Samuel 7:18, 20):

King David sat before the Lord and said:

“Who am I, O Lord God?”

“What is my family that you have brought me thus far?”

“What more can David say to you? You know your servant, O Lord God”

The Lesson: With all humility, David sits before the Lord, acknowledging his unworthiness and God’s sovereign choice.

THE MESSIANIC TITLE: “SON OF DAVID”

The Blind Bartimaeus (Mark 10:47-48):

When the blind man heard that Jesus of Nazareth was passing by, he cried out: “Jesus, Son of David, have mercy on me!”

Significant Detail: Many rebuked him to keep quiet, but he shouted all the louder: “Son of David, have mercy on me!”

Theological Significance: He called Jesus “Son of David”—not “son of Mary” or “son of Joseph”—recognizing the Messianic lineage.

GOD’S TESTIMONY: “A MAN AFTER MY OWN HEART”

We hear about David from Prophet Samuel’s Declaration (1 Samuel 13:14): “The Lord has chosen someone else more pleasing to Him whom He has appointed to be Commander over His people”

Paul’s Testimony (Acts 13:22): Paul says David was man whose heart was inclined towards God. “I have found David the son of Jesse, a man after my own heart, who will do all I want him to do”

The Divine Promise: David will do not a little—he will do everything God wants him to do.

WHY WAS DAVID CALLED “A MAN AFTER GOD’S OWN HEART”?

The Context of David’s Anointing

God’s Rejection of Saul: God rejected King Saul for his disobedience and asked Samuel to anoint someone in Jesse’s family in Saul’s place—while Saul was still ruling the kingdom.

The Anointing Scene (1 Samuel 16:12-13):

Jesse presented six sons before Samuel—the Lord rejected all of them

Samuel asked: “Are these all your children?”

Jesse replied: “I have another son, David. He is tending the flock in the forest”

Samuel commanded: “Call for that boy”

When David was brought before him, the Lord said to Samuel: “Go, anoint him, for this is the one”

Critical Detail: David was only 13 years old and still a shepherd boy.

Divine Indwelling: “From that day onwards, the Lord’s Spirit took hold of David” (1 Samuel 16:13)

God’s Declaration (Acts 13:22): David was still a young boy, yet God says: “David is after my own heart, and therefore I have chosen him to be king”

What David Had NOT Yet Done:

He had not yet fought with Amalekites or Philistines

He had not yet killed Goliath

He was just a simple shepherd boy

Yet God Declared: “He is close to my heart because he will do everything I desire”

FIVE FOUNDATIONAL LESSONS FROM DAVID’S LIFE

LESSON ONE: THE HEART MATTERS MORE THAN APPEARANCE

The Divine Selection Principle:

David is chosen to be king of Israel because he had what Saul did not have: the heart for God.

Prophet Samuel’s Insight in (1 Samuel 13:14): “The Lord sought out a man after His own heart to be king, and King Saul simply was not this man”

The Anointing Story tells us: When Samuel went to anoint the new king of Israel, he assumed that David’s strong, noble, and oldest brother must be the chosen one.

But God’s Instruction to Samuel (1 Samuel 16:7) was: “Do not judge by his looks or his stature, for I have rejected him. The Lord does not judge as humans judge. Humans look at the outer appearance, but the Lord sees the heart.”

Why did God skip the tall brothers and pick shepherd boy David? Because He wasn't looking for a king with a big stature—He wanted one with a big heart! Saul had the height but not the height of faith.

Application:

Impressive resumes of skills, talents, lifestyle, gadgets, show casing talents and credentials are not what make us useful in God’s Kingdom

The importance of cultivating a heart for God above all else

LESSON TWO: COURAGE, FAITHFULNESS, AND LOVE

The Lion Incident (1 Samuel 17:36-37): this denotes David’s character

While David was tending his father’s sheep, a lion took away a lamb from the flock. David’s response:

He ran after the lion

He snatched the lamb from the lion’s mouth

When the lion jumped to attack, David caught the lion by its beard and slew it

What This Reveals About David’s Character:

This event tells us about the Great courage in the face of danger

It tells us of David’s Love for that lamb (willing to risk his life)

David’s Faithfulness to the duty of tending his father’s flock

David’s Own Testimony: “I have killed lions and bears, and will do the same with this uncircumcised Philistine. The Lord who delivered me from the paws of lions and bears will deliver me from the hands of the Philistine” (1 Samuel 17:36-37)

This displays David’s faith in his God and his personal relationship with the Lord. We should develop such a personal relationship and trust in God.

Reflection Question: How much time do we personally spend with the Lord in the secret place? It determines your spirituality.

LESSON THREE: HUMILITY IN SERVICE—NO TASK TOO MENIAL

The Food Delivery Assignment:

David’s father Jesse one day asked the young lad to go with food and seek his brothers in the battlefield, saying: “Hurry to the camp”

David’s Potential Response: David was already anointed king. He could have said:

“Hereafter I will not tend the sheep”

“I will not carry food to my brothers”

“I am an anointed king!”

David’s Actual Response: He did not think it was a menial task beneath his ability and position. He obeyed without complaint.

If David had refused, thinking it beneath him, he would have lost his great moment of opportunity.

Theological Truth is: It is difficult to understand God’s favourable time. You may be doing a very ordinary job, but no matter what you do, do it with your heart. God will surely give you your moment of opportunity.

Biblical Foundation (1 Peter 5:6): “Humble yourself under the mighty hand of God”

A seminarian once prayed: “Lord, use me in a mighty way—give me a big mission, a big pulpit, a big crowd!” It was as if the Lord replied: “First, can I trust you with washing the plates, sweeping the corridor, and visiting that one lonely person?” Moral punchline: God usually tests us with “sheep and tiffin-carriers” before He gives us Goliaths.

Application:

Whatever ministry you are assigned to

Whatever you are commissioned to do

Be ready to do it responsibly, faithfully, and joyfully

The Lord will raise you up at the appointed time

LESSON FOUR: RESPONSIBILITY AND MATURITY

The Morning Routine (1 Samuel 17:20):

Israel and the Philistines faced off in the Valley of Elah, with the giant Goliath taunting Israel twice daily for 40 days, challenging them to single combat and demoralizing Saul’s army. David, the youngest son of Jesse from Bethlehem, had previously served Saul as a musician but returned to shepherding.

Jesse sends David (~15-20 years old) with food for his three older brothers in the army, to check on them and bring back news.

“David woke up early the next morning and left the sheep with someone and went off as his father had commanded him”

A young brother told his rector: “Father, I’m ready for any mission—Africa, Europe, America, wherever God sends me!” The rector smiled and said, “Wonderful. For a start, God is sending you… to the library at 6 am to put the books back in order.” The brother replied, “Father, I said I’m ready to go anywhere for God—just not so near and so early!”

“David didn’t argue about ‘how far’ or ‘how early.’ He simply did as his father commanded him.”

What This Shows:

Responsible behaviour: He didn’t abandon the sheep

Matured behaviour: He made proper arrangements

Obedient spirit: He did “as his father had commanded him”

Jesus’ Principle (Luke 16:10): “If you are faithful in small things, you will be faithful in greater things”

What is our Contemporary Challenge:

It is in our culture to do things differently when someone is watching or not watching. This is our common mistake:

When there is no camera on the highways, people drive very fast

If our superiors and leaders are watching us, we behave differently

Do we check WhatsApp messages and emails during work/study in the college?

David’s Example: “As his father commanded him”—he woke up early and ran to his brothers, whether anyone was watching or not.

Goliath taunted Israel for 40 days, but no one budged. David arrives and asks, “Who's this giant trash-talking my God?” Brothers snap, “Go back to your sheep!” David replies, “Sheep don't defy Yahweh—apparently, you do!” Moral: Sometimes the real giants are our own indifference.

LESSON FIVE: ZEAL FOR GOD’S GLORY

The Goliath Challenge

The Situation (1 Samuel 17:16):

Goliath: a nine-feet-tall, strong, and powerful Philistine

Standing on the other side of the mountain

Challenging the Israelites to fight with him

For 40 days, morning and evening, Goliath was challenging Saul’s army

The Response of Israel’s Army:

Neither King Saul nor any of his generals could take up the challenge

They were just sitting and watching

Goliath was even humiliating the God of Yahweh

But no one dared to respond

Israel’s Condition:

Saul’s army was a well-trained army

They had defeated many other nations

But here Saul was lethargic and terrified (1 Samuel 17:11)

“When they heard this challenge of the Philistine, Saul and his men were dejected and terrified”

The Modern Parallel

Our Comfortable Complacency: This is what is happening even today in our life:

“Why should we bother about others’ issues?”

“We have our comforts”

“We have everything we want”

“We get up in the morning slowly, say some prayers, have our food”

“We have a job assigned to us—we do the minimum and nothing extra”

That is our way of living

Albert Einstein’s Warning: “The world will not be destroyed by those who do evil, but by those who watch the evil without doing anything”

David’s Holy Disturbance

The Context:

God chose the Israel people and helped them occupy a country

Now Goliath is blaspheming God

But no one bothered about it

No one was ashamed of this challenge

Who Was Disturbed? The young lad of 17, David, tending cattle in the wilderness—it was he who was disturbed by the happenings around him.

David’s Response: He had the zeal and shame to question: “Who is this Goliath to talk against my God? How can he criticize my God?”

David’s Family Background

The Forgotten Son: When God sent Samuel to anoint a king, Jesse (David’s father) presented before him only his first six children. He didn’t even remember his seventh son, David. He thought: “That last one is just tending the flock and he is too young”

But the Lord anointed him.

David’s Reflection (Psalm): “Even if my parents forget me, my God is there. My God is there.”

The Brothers’ Rebuke

David brought food to his brothers in the army. These brothers never bothered about Goliath who was challenging them, but they even humiliated David (1 Samuel 17:28):

The Brothers’ Questions:

“Why did you come here to roam around?”

“With whom did you leave the sheep in the desert?”

“You are an insolent fellow!”

“You have come only to watch the battle!”

The Irony: As if those brothers were fighting against the Philistines! They rebuked David, the young lad.

David’s Deep Pain: David felt deep pain because a human (Goliath) was challenging his living God.

When Will We Get Disturbed?

Reflection Questions:

When are we going to get disturbed like this by common issues? We don’t need to worry about great things. We are not being asked to fight against powerful men like Goliath.

About Our Faithfulness: Yet how faithful and creative are we in carrying out what is assigned to us?

About Our Passion:

How passionate are we in God’s matters?

Or how indifferent are we?

How much time do we spend for Him and listen to Him?

What is our intimacy, our relationship with our God?

How do we live for Him?

About Our Integrity: No one needs to see us or watch us. When David was tending the sheep, no one saw him. He was faithful to his God Yahweh.

Saul’s Discouragement

Even King Saul discouraged David to fight against Goliath, saying that a young boy like him could not fight against the mighty Goliath who has been a warrior since his youth.

David’s Answer (1 Samuel 17:36): “I fought with lions and saved my sheep, and will do the same with this uncircumcised Philistine, for he has defiled the armies of the Living God”

EXAMPLES OF HOLY DISTURBANCE THROUGHOUT HISTORY

Biblical Examples

John the Baptist: Couldn’t keep quiet seeing the unlawful behaviour of Herod

St. Thomas More: Refused to listen to Henry VIII of England and was killed for his conviction

Modern Examples

William Wilberforce: The British parliamentarian fought against the African slave trade and finally abolished it by law

Martin Luther King Jr.: Fought against discrimination of blacks and racism

Mother Teresa: Everybody saw poverty in Kolkata, but only Mother Teresa was disturbed

Nelson Mandela: Saw the racial cruelty in South Africa, and for raising his voice, he was jailed for 27 years

Fr. Stan Swamy, SJ: This human rights activist couldn’t remain a silent spectator when tribals and Dalits were unjustly denied their rights in Jharkhand (a state in North India). He was jailed and he died in the jail for standing for truth and justice.

The Common Thread among these heroes.

These people could not remain muted spectators. They plunged into action and changed the situation.

The Principle: Whatever disturbs God should disturb us too. I can’t leave this struggling, suffering society as it is. I want to change it in my own capacity. We all must get this zeal and focus—then we can do wonders.

THE POWER OF ORDINARY PEOPLE WITH EXTRAORDINARY ZEAL

Jesus’ Example:

Jesus chose 12 men who were:

Ordinary

Illiterate

Without modern facilities like internet, microphones, mobile phones, emails, etc.

The Result: They changed the world after Pentecost.

The Divine Strategy: Sometimes God puts a Goliath in our life to find the David within us and bring out that zeal lying dormant within us.

A young seminarian was complaining in prayer: “Lord, my life is full of giants—deadlines, difficult people, problems everywhere. Why are You sending me so many Goliaths?”

God gently replied: “My son, I am not sending you Goliaths to crush you. I am sending them to wake up the David who is sleeping inside you.”

“Sometimes God puts a Goliath in our life to find the David within us and bring out that zeal lying dormant within us.”

CONCLUSION: LIVING TO GOD’S CHOICE

The Ultimate Question: When are we going to live to God’s choice?

The Divine Promise: Only then the Lord can say to us: “You are close to my heart”

Personal Application: Only then the Lord can say to us also: “You are close to my heart”

CLOSING NOTE

There are still a few more lessons we can learn from the life of David. This reflection will continue tomorrow too.

Summary of Key Lessons for Theological Reflection:

The Heart Over Appearance - God sees what humans cannot see

Courage, Faithfulness, and Love - Tested in the wilderness, proven in battle

Humility in Service - No task too menial for the anointed

Responsibility and Maturity - Faithful in small things, faithful in great things

Zeal for God’s Glory - Holy disturbance that leads to holy action

Questions for Personal Reflection:

What disturbs me about the state of the Church and world today?

Am I faithful when no one is watching?

Do I see ordinary tasks as beneath me, or as opportunities for God’s glory?

How much time do I spend in personal intimacy with God?

Am I willing to be disturbed enough to act?

“I have found David the son of Jesse, a man after my own heart, who will do all I want him to do” (Acts 13:22)

May we, like David, be men and women after God’s own heart.

Lord our God, we thank You for the life of Your servant David, for his humble heart, his courage, and his zeal for Your glory.

Give us too a heart after Your own heart: a heart that listens to Your word, repents quickly when it falls, trusts You in every Goliath we face, and serves You faithfully in small and great things alike.

Create in us a clean heart, O God, renew a steadfast spirit within us, and help us to live each day so that You may say of us also: “You are close to my heart.”

We ask this through Jesus, the Son of David and our Good Shepherd. Amen.

https://youtu.be/u0zggta4oeE part-1

https://youtu.be/NkAVG6hzsik part-2

KING DAVID PART II: A MAN AFTER GOD’S OWN HEART

A Biblical Recollection for Theologians (Continued)

LESSON SIX: DISCOVER YOUR UNIQUENESS

David’s Declaration to Goliath (1 Samuel 17:45)

David answered the Philistine Goliath: “You come against me with sword, spear, and javelin, but I come against you in the name of the Living God whom you have defied”

The Contrast:

Goliath: came with all weapons

David: came with only one weapon—the name of the Living God whom you have defied

The Battle Belongs to the Lord

Moses’ Encouragement (Exodus 14:14): “The battle is the Lord’s. He will do all the fighting for you”

Theological Truth:

The battle is God’s battle

Our problems are His problems

We think we have to do all the fighting

David’s Five Stones (1 Samuel 17:40)

The Biblical Account: The young boy David picked up five stones from the brook. He threw just one stone out of the five.

The Reality: It is the Lord who used this stone, and not David

The Trap of Comparison

Our Common Mistake: We normally compare ourselves with others and get defeated, saying:

“I can’t talk like you”

“I can’t preach like you”

“I don’t have the courage to fight for justice like you”

“I don’t have the charism to conduct prayer like you”

“I can’t live an upright life like you”

This is what our comparison with others does to us.

You Are Unique

The Divine Truth: Remember: You are unique. After creating you, God destroyed the mold.

St. Paul’s Teaching (Ephesians 2:10): “What we are is God’s handiwork. Each one of us is God’s art, handiwork”

The Psalmist’s Beauty (Psalm 139:13): “You knit me together in my mother’s womb”

God’s Creativity: We are created in an amazing way. Even in the same family, each one is different.

Learning from Others Without Losing Yourself

The Balance:

You can take their good spirit if you find somebody a good example

If somebody you want to emulate, you can take the spirit

But you cannot be like someone else

God doesn’t want us to be like someone else

The Principle: Each one is gifted with unique talents. You develop them and serve the Lord without comparison and competition.

Practical Application

Love What You Have:

Love what you have

Love the mission assigned to you

You run your race

Take your space

Don’t look at your neighbor

Don’t spoil your life by comparing with others

The Truth: My goodness doesn’t depend upon the goodness of others

The Command: You be you

LESSON SEVEN: THE ZEAL OF DAVID FOR THE HOUSE OF THE LORD

Saul’s Jealousy After David’s Victory

The Expected Response: Since David defeated Goliath and saved the country, King Saul should have appreciated David. There was no personal problem between King Saul and David.

The Reality: What Saul was supposed to do to save the country by fighting against Goliath, the young lad David did and saved his country.

The Women’s Song (1 Samuel 18:6-7)

After David Defeated Goliath: Women came out singing and dancing with tambourines and cries of joy:

“Saul has killed his thousands, and David his ten thousands”

Critical Detail: It’s not David who sang—women on the street were singing this.

The Consequence: This singing of women created enmity. Saul became jealous. He could not bear David’s popularity. He began the search to kill David.

Saul’s Relentless Pursuit

The Obsession: King Saul spent 13 years and used 3,000 soldiers to kill David—as if killing David was his only mission as a king.

David’s Psalms During Persecution

During These Tense Moments: See how David was able to write so many Psalms!

We read is Psalm 27:4

“One thing I asked of the Lord: to live in the house of the Lord all the days of my life”

And the same Psalm 27:8-9

“Your face, Lord, do I seek. Do not hide your face from me”

David’s God-Experience: See the God-experience of this young boy:

“I want to live in your presence”

“Problems and difficulties are there, but I need you”

As we read in Psalm 84:1

“How lovely is your dwelling place, O Lord”

Psalm 84:10

“For a day in your courts is better than a thousand elsewhere. I would rather be a doorkeeper in the house of my God”

David’s Priority: “I would rather be a watchman, a doorkeeper in the house of my God”

Reflection Question: Can we get this burning zeal for God?

LESSON EIGHT: THE NOBILITY OF DAVID

David’s Innocent Question (1 Samuel 20:1)

To Jonathan (Saul’s Son): Burning with jealousy, Saul had tried more than once to kill David. Tired of trying to escape from King Saul, David asked Saul’s son Jonathan:

“What have I done, Jonathan? What am I guilty of? What sin have I committed against your father that he wants to kill me?”

The Truth: There is no reason for Saul to kill him except jealousy.

Theological Principle: Suffering Without Cause

The Reality: Adversity and troubles are not always punishment for evil. We will not always have an immediate explanation as to why we have been dealt with so harshly—in spite of doing good and being good.

The Mystery: Many times, God does what He does simply because He is who He is, and offers no explanation, as it was in the case of Job.

David’s Restraint (1 Samuel 24:6-7)

The Opportunity: Yet we see the nobility of David. Even when he had an opportunity to kill Saul, David restrained himself and his servants, saying:

“The Lord forbid that I should do this thing to my lord, the Lord’s anointed, to raise my hand against him”

David’s Repeated Declaration: David repeatedly says: “For King Saul is the Lord’s anointed”

David’s Magnanimity: We admire the magnanimity of David. He could have easily taken revenge and retaliation by killing Saul, who was always looking to kill him.

David’s Regret (1 Samuel 24:6): David even regretted for cutting the edge of Saul’s cloak.

The Christian Response to Injustice

The Challenge: Injustice is one of the most difficult things for any of us to bear.

The Wrong Reaction: One type of reaction toward those who treat us unjustly is to retaliate.

The Truth: However, retaliation is unwise and unchristlike.

David’s Example: David demonstrated great patience and love toward King Saul, even though Saul was consistently trying to take David’s life.

Jesus’ Teaching in Action:

David walked the second mile

He turned the other cheek

This is the attitude taught by Jesus in the New Testament

The Principle: If people are mean and jealous and judgmental, it is their problem. It reveals their behaviour. Being magnanimous and noble is a divine character.

Historical Example: St. Augustine and St. Jerome

The Context: Here I remember the small event recorded in the life of St. Augustine of Hippo. St. Augustine and St. Jerome were contemporaries.

The Conflict: Once Jerome was in conflict with Augustine and wrote to him in anger: “Augustine, I know who you are. I know your past.”

St. Augustine’s Magnanimous Reply: “I know that God’s grace which has prevented you from sinning saved me. I know that God’s grace prevented you from sinning. The same God’s grace saved me from sin.”

What a magnanimous reply!

David’s Mourning for Saul (2 Samuel 1:1, 11-12) when Saul himself died

When Saul Died: Finally, King Saul died a miserable death.

David’s Unexpected Response: David did not rejoice. Instead:

He took hold of his clothes and tore them

He mourned and wept and fasted

Understanding David’s Grief:

When Jonathan (David’s close friend) also died, David’s mourning was understandable

But it’s difficult to comprehend David’s grief for Saul

It can only be understood with the spark lit by divine grace

David’s Grief for Absalom (2 Samuel 19:1-2)

The Betrayal: When Absalom, David’s own son, turned against his father David:

He chased his father David to assert the kingdom

Absalom got killed

David’s Response: David was greatly disturbed. Again, he wept and said:

“My son Absalom! My son, my son Absalom! If I had died instead of you! O Absalom, my son, my son!”

The Report: “Thus it was reported: Joab, the king is weeping and mourning for Absalom”

The Power of Forgiveness

The Principle:

Revenge only spirals violence

But forgiveness disables it

The Challenge: However, forgiving is never easy.

The Truth:

Only the resilient and strong can forgive

The rigid and the weak retaliate

Forgiving is not weakness

Indeed, forgiveness is the most formidable form of revenge

Understanding Jealousy

The Root: Jealousy kills. It comes from one’s own insecurity.

The Problem: When I compare myself with the other person:

I fail to see my own worth

I feel uncomfortable with what others are

The Illustration: Each star in the sky is unique. No star feels jealous or wants to destroy the other.

Good Exercise: It is a good exercise to see the sky at night.

Let’s Reflect St. Paul’s Divine Experience (Romans 8:31)

“If God is for us, who can be against us?”

“If God is for us, who can be against us?”

LESSON NINE: EVEN THE STRONGEST CAN FALL

David’s Sin with Bathsheba

The Context: Even though David was a powerful king over Israel for 40 years, and his heart was definitely inclined Godward, he too was not without faults.

The Sin: He was attracted to Bathsheba, Uriah’s wife. In order to marry her, he planned to kill Uriah.

The Murder Plot:

Uriah was part of David’s army

David instructed the head of his army, Joab, to have him positioned in the front line of the battle so as to get him killed

David made Bathsheba his wife

The Double Crime: David committed the double crime of being an adulterer and a murderer.

The Shocking Reality

The Contrast: After hearing so many great qualities of David, and as the man after God’s own heart, this event in David’s life comes as a rude shock.

God’s Response: God wasn’t pleased by David’s acts and sent Nathan the prophet to convey His wrath to King David.

David’s Immediate Repentance

When Confronted: David, after being confronted by the prophet, acknowledged his crime.

Realizing and Owning His Mistakes: David repented and confessed immediately:

“I have sinned against the Lord”

David’s Honesty:

He did not compromise

He did not give any reason

He did not try to hide his faults

“I have sinned against the Lord”

Psalm 51: And he wrote the popular Psalm 51, acknowledging his weaknesses publicly.

LESSONS FROM DAVID’S DARK PERIOD

Two Critical Warnings

Warning One: Beware of Idleness

Warning Two: Beware of Thinking “It Can’t Happen to Me”

The Glorious Reality of God’s Mercy

The Caution: David’s life teaches us not to take the glorious reality of God’s mercy for granted.

The Sobering Truth: Even the strongest person can easily slip:

At any moment

At any hour

Anybody

Anywhere

Can be caught off guard

And can fail God miserably

Yet, We Have a Forgiving God

The Hope: Yet, let’s not forget that we have a God who:

Understands our frailties and weaknesses

Forgives and forgets our sins

When we acknowledge our sinfulness and repent

Isaiah 38:17: “He casts all our sins behind His back” as we read in Isaiah 38:17:

He casts all our sins behind His back

The Invitation: Let’s return to Him and receive compassionate forgiveness and be determined to walk in His ways always.

CONCLUSION: A MAN AFTER GOD’S OWN HEART

God’s Testimony Repeated

Acts 13:22: “David, a man after my own heart, who will do all I want him to do”

David’s Declaration (Acts 2:25)

David spoke of God when he said:

“I saw the Lord always before me. Since He is at my right hand, I will not be shaken”

“I saw the Lord always before me. Since He is at my right hand, I will not be shaken”

God Works Through Weak and Sinful Persons

The Encouragement: David’s life is a prime example in the Scriptures of how God works through weak, frail, and sinful persons like you and me to accomplish His purpose.

God’s Only Expectation: He only expects us to be ready to do all that He wants us to do.

The Call to Return

The Question: Can we return to the Lord, acknowledging our weaknesses and failures?

The Promise: He will never forsake us.

What a God We Have: Such an awful, wonderful, forgiving, compassionate God we have!

FINAL REFLECTION QUESTIONS

The Personal Challenge

Question One: “A man after my own heart”—Can God write a statement about you and me like this?

Question Two: “A man after my own heart”—Can God write a statement about you and me like this?

Three Deep Questions for Meditation

Reflection 1: Why did God call David “a man after my own heart”?

Why did God call David “a man after my own heart”?

Reflection 2: How is David’s life challenging your life?

Reflection 3: What are the concrete lessons you can learn from his life?

The Purpose: Every person is placed in the Bible to learn lessons. What lessons is David’s life teaching us?

CLOSING PRAYER

Our compassionate, loving, benevolent God,

We thank You for King David. We thank You for having placed him in the Scriptures. We thank You for the beautiful lessons we were able to glean from his life.

Thank You, Lord, for strengthening us in our good moments and weak moments.

You are a loving God. At the same time, You are a forgiving God.

Help us to acknowledge our weaknesses and to return to You.

We want to be close to Your heart. We want to be known as after Your own heart.

We are Your children, loving God. We are at Your disposal. Make use of us. Whatever mission that You would like to give us, may we ask Your strength and Your grace.

In the name of Jesus Christ Our Lord. Amen.

A man who was:

Chosen by God despite his youth and obscurity

Courageous against lions, bears, and giants

Humble in service and success

Zealous for God’s glory and God’s house

Unique in his gifts and calling

Magnanimous toward his enemies

Fallible yet immediately repentant

Forgiven and restored by God’s mercy
